#6971. Photographer Jessica Craig-Martin’s New Memoir Reveals the Life Behind Her “Drunken Lens”

Her new memoir, I Regret I Am Able to Attend, is out now

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Since the early ’90s, photographer Jessica Craig-Martin has captured high society from unusual angles. She zooms in on air kisses and other mannered gestures, cleavage in couture gowns, high heels, and the floors typically omitted from pristine event documentation, straddling the line between fashion photography and art portraiture.

Craig-Martin’s memoir, I Regret I Am Able to Attend, out now, tells the story of how she got there. Craig-Martin details her bohemian childhood, wild friendships, and volatile young marriage with the effervescence that fills her compositions.

The story begins with the photographer’s parents, artists Michael Craig-Martin and Jan Hashey, who meet at the Yale School of Art. They have Jessica, then swiftly separate after revelations that they’re both having affairs, and Michael comes out as gay. From there, Craig-Martin experiences a peripatetic childhood, eventually settling in New York in her mother’s SoHo loft. Her creative milieu includes gallerist Paula Cooper and artists Elizabeth Murray, Jennifer Bartlett, and Joel Shapiro, along with their children. Craig-Martin gets a job at high-end grocer Dean & Deluca, where Basquiat comes in to buy smoked eel for a strange supper. Artists Brice and Helen Marden hire her to babysit their daughters, and soon she’s off to Morocco and Greece with the family.

Instead of attending college, Craig-Martin receives an alternative education as she lands a gig waitressing at Brian McNally’s celebrity-filled restaurant Indochine. She flies to London to start a job as a fashion assistant at Anna Wintour’s British Vogue (where they love her American enthusiasm), then back in New York, she works at Tina Brown’s Vanity Fair (where they love her British sophistication). Throughout the memoir, Craig-Martin captures the colorful characters who parade in and out of her life and around a rapidly evolving New York City.

Ahead of the book’s publication and her forthcoming show at the Andres Thalmann gallery in Paris, Bazaar spoke to Craig-Martin about her writing process and her shifting understanding of gender in the art world. We were on the telephone, and the artist was visiting “I Am Plane Image,” a show of Brice Marden’s paintings at Gagosian in Chelsea. A guard reprimanded her for getting too close to a painting. “When I was a kid, one was encouraged to touch the art,” she told me. “A lot of art was activated by the viewer. And now someone’s always saying, ‘Excuse me!’ ‘Excuse me!’ ”

You’re a society photographer whose pictures conjure an entire milieu. Your book does something similar, but with language. How do you think about the relationship between writing and photography?

It’s the same brain, making different things. With photography, I never thought about what I was going to make before I made it. I’d see something with my camera, and I’d have to grab it. It’s a wonderful feeling of adrenaline, and it’s similar to looking for the next word. Everything just disappears. There’s a loss of self in the process, and you become your purest self.

In the book, your aesthetic education seems to come, in part, from painter Brice Marden.

With the camera, I felt a certain freedom. I really didn’t know much about photography, because it wasn’t a popular medium with my parents and their friends. And from Brice, I understood this freedom to put what you want in a picture. You can leave things out, push it around until it works, make it as weird as you want. If you feel like cutting off the top of a head in a photograph, try it!

I knew certain photographers, but not society photographers like Tina Barney and Larry Fink. I only knew a few pictures of Weegee’s. There was a lot for me to discover. If I’d seen more of this work, I would have felt inadequate. In this case, ignorance was bliss. Academic training can make you self-conscious. I didn’t have anything to unlearn.

Your mother has a philosophy that artists are the real aristocracy. Do you share it?

She was right about everything she said. But it is extreme. I mean, we want doctors and we need other professions. But I think all artists believe this, as did all of us art brats who grew up in this kind of crazy, free-range situation. Art comes first. Everything else comes afterwards. It’s what we are in the truest form.

Good work rises to the top. Then again, not everyone whose work is good has been correctly honored or addressed. Women and people of color were left out of the discourse.

Your mother also says that Indochine is where you became a photographer.

I was raised in the very lean ’60s and ’70s, and then suddenly New York just was drunk on money overnight. And this job threw me into the middle of it. It was a hot seat to view all this excess. I found my subject matter there. Everything’s moving at once, and I keep that feeling when I’m taking a photograph. There’s a lot of motion in my pictures, and I’m capturing the feet or I’m above the person or I’m not where a photographer would normally be. [Gallerist] Jeanne Greenberg calls it my “drunken lens.”

The book is also a portrait of an evolving New York. It’s more sanitized now.

New York is still New York. We lose a lot with the corporatization of the city. But that’s global. It’s the same fuckers in every city making it into glass, concrete, steel with a bank in the bottom of the building and a Starbucks. It’s more painful here because New York is my city. But you can’t be too nostalgic because it’s also about speed and change. If you get stuck, you’re missing the point.

The sex appeal is now in Brooklyn. I’m an old fart now, I don’t hang out, but I know there are young people who are opening galleries and making good art. It’s just harder and harder to find a crack in the sidewalk to grow out of.

Toward the end of the book, during your marriage to artist Ashley Bickerton, you write that you made “Art Wife Curry.” That comes shortly after your mention of Rirkrit Tiravanija making curry at 303 Gallery, which he just calls “art.” You write about the covert sexism in the art world versus the overt sexism in the restaurant world. In the writing process, did you come to think any differently about how gender had shaped you?

Yeah. When you’re naive and you’re a young woman, you’re experiencing these invisible forces pushing you around, or men are behaving in a certain way, and you’re used to it. It’s like air. You don’t see it. And not just writing the book, but getting older, you can really see the forces that pulled you this way or that, where you felt a limitation. When I look back, I think, Why didn’t I just say “fuck you”? Or why didn’t I say, No, no, I’m coming through? Women second-guess themselves a great deal more than men. Men push and then afterwards say, Oh, did I break it? And then everyone claps. That’s not the classic woman’s story. There are corrections happening, and many of them are very well-deserved.

What kinds of limitations are you thinking about?

It’s about confidence. Being an unquestioning force in the world and feeling like you have a right to be seen on the wall, have just as big a studio as your husband or partner. If you go to the Krasner Pollock home in Springs in Long Island, his studio was famously the barn, a giant empty space. And hers was this tiny room. That would be the normal order of things. That’s the way it was in my family, even though I thought of my parents as liberated. My mother was considered a star at Yale, and as soon as she left and had a baby, she was a mother first, artist second. My parents’ generation thought they had dealt with a lot of this second-classing of women, and they really hadn’t.

The book ends when you’re around 35, your marriage is over, and your photography career has finally begun. Why end there? Will there be a Volume 2?

Good question. There might be. But I’ve also told my friends to tie me up and duct tape me to a wall if I ever start to try, because this was so difficult to make. I’m already forgetting how dreadful I found the experience.

#6966. ‘Off Campus’ Season 2 Might Make a Major Departure From the Books, Thanks to This Casting Shakeup

The absence could change a key storyline from the books in the Prime Video series.

#6962. Do Monsters Lurk Around Us in Plain Sight? Gentle Monster’s Director Worries They Do

Bazaar spoke with Austrian director Marie Kreutzer about her Cannes favorite starring Léa Seydoux and Laurence Rupp

A newspaper clipping about a ring of pedophiles captivated the attention of Austrian filmmaker Marie Kreutzer one morning six years ago. The article detailed the buildup and the arrest in explicit detail, but one description felt more haunting than the rest.

“It was shocking because most of the guys in that ring of criminals were normal family dads,” Kreutzer tells Bazaar on a recent call. “It made me so much more aware of how close these perpetrators are, more than we want to think, and that I must almost certainly also know one.… When I read that article, and it described how the police officers were standing at the doorsteps of these guys, my first thought was, ‘How would that be for me if it were my husband?’ ”

Kreutzer decided to channel this shock, the all-consuming fear that we cannot trust those we love the most, into her latest film, Gentle Monster. Releasing on Netflix on November 18, the film tells the story of Lucy Weiss (Léa Seydoux), an experimental pianist who leaves her life behind to raise her young son with her doe-eyed husband, Philip (Laurence Rupp). The trio exists happily in the German countryside, complete with all the comforts of a cozy domestic life. Lucy and Philip love each other and their son, enjoying a sex-filled marriage that appears to have only the regular bumps. This illusion is disrupted when police knock on the door, arresting Philip for his alleged participation in a child pornography ring. Kruetzer designed the couple introduction to feel as if the rug were pulled out from under the viewer.

“It was obviously very important to have all these scenes where they interact before everything happens and where we see that they have a very natural, loving relationship,” she says. “For me, [Rupp] has nothing secretive or mysterious about him. He’s very there. And that’s what I wanted for the role, because I thought it would be good to see him and never really be open to believing that that guy would really do something like that.” She remembered telling Rupp, “It’s not like you’re playing a petty criminal. You’re playing a liar.” Kruetzer wanted him to “make us want to come back to you.”

In this way, Rupp embodies an uncanny confidence throughout the entire movie. He, of course, carries a sense of guilt, yet an audience can’t easily parse out the weight of his internal feelings. Kruetzer also refrains from giving too much direction about his innocence or guilt. Even when he is released from prison the first time, his nonchalance diffuses any skepticism; you might believe him when he says he has nothing to do with it. And when evidence mounts, including actual files, he twists the story again, telling Lucy he did it for the money. Rupp’s sympathetic portrayal is confusing—enough so that you can understand Lucy’s turmoil. Kruetzer remembers telling him to “play it as if you just messed up the taxes,” and later he told her, “I didn’t really play the monster; I played the gentle.”

This portrayal adds weight to Seydoux’s indelible performance as Lucy. The entire film orbits her reaction to this horrific revelation. Seydoux’s anger and forgiveness oscillate at a frenetic pace throughout the movie, pulling the viewer into Lucy’s point of view, as well as what Kreutzer first felt when she read the newspaper clipping.

Casting Seydoux, the director says, felt intuitive. The French actress dove headfirst into the script, expressing great interest in the character and the story. “The subject matter is tough, but the fact that it’s through the woman’s perspective—and not through the victim or perpetrator—it’s through her emotions that the story unfolds [is what interested me],” Seydoux says in Bazaar’s profile from last week. One bone-rattling scene in particular, where Lucy faces off with Philip in a light rain and directly confronts him about his arrest, underscores why she’s considered one of the best living French actresses. “As soon as we turned the camera towards her, it was really surprising what came out of her and what poured out of her,” Kreutzer said. “I remember that the crew was in shock.”

The main storyline provides enough anxiety and suspense, yet Kreutzer wrote a side plot for Gentle Monster to offer perspective. One of the cops who knocks on the Weiss family door, Elsa (Jella Haase), provides a removed point of view on Lucy’s internal dilemma. The character provides a broader understanding of the crime at the center of this story, emphasizing how widespread and hidden this problem is. “For Lucy, her life is falling apart because of this, whereas for Elsa, it’s just one of many cases, and it’s not even a bad one,” Kreutzer says. “It was important for me to show the magnitude of the problem and to not let the audience lean back and think, ‘Okay, that’s very dramatic, but it has nothing to do with me.’ ”

#6961. Who Is Daddy Franklin From ‘The Body’? For Starters, Nnamdi Asomugha Is Kerry Washington’s Husband

Daddy Franklin is married to Olivia Pope?

If you watched The Body in one sitting and are now fully obsessed with it, you’re not alone!! The horrifying coming-of-age series is scary good, and while you may not be familiar with the actor who plays Father Samuel Franklin (aka Daddy Franklin), you definitely know his very famous wife. As it turns out, Nnamdi Asomugha has been married to Kerry Washington for over 13 years now, having fallen in love after watching Kerry perform in Race on Broadway in 2010. Kerry later confirmed the story during a 2018 interview with Marie Claire, saying, “The last time I did theater, it completely transformed my life. That’s where I met my husband.” Cute!!!

Though things got off to a rocky start (Kerry apparently told Nnamdi she didn’t date athletes, and Nnamdi told Kerry he didn’t date actors), the two got together anyway, and just about three years later in June 2013, they tied the knot in a super low-key ceremony. According to their marriage license, Kerry and Nnamdi said “I do” in Hailey, Idaho, where Nnamdi’s sister officiated and Kerry’s parents served as witnesses. Since then, the couple has welcomed two kids together—Isabelle Amarachi, born in 2014, and Caleb Kelechi, born in 2016—and are seemingly thriving outside of the spotlight. But we think it’s time to learn a lil bit more about Nnamdi, don’t you?

Here’s everything you should know about the man behind Daddy Franklin, including how he rose to fame as a former NFL cornerback and what he’s up to next.

After growing up in Louisiana in a Nigerian Igbo household, Nnamdi attended the University of California, Berkeley, where he played for the California Golden Bears football team. Soon after graduating, Nnamdi was drafted in the first round of the 2003 NFL Draft by the Oakland Raiders, which is very impressive.

Over his 11-year professional football career, Nnamdi also played for the Philadelphia Eagles and the San Francisco 49ers, and in December 2013, the cornerback signed a one-day contract with Oakland so he could retire as a Raider. I love to see a loyal man!

According to Nnamdi, the decision to retire at 32 was difficult, but it still felt like the right time for him. “Yeah, I felt that it was a good time, but it didn’t make it any easier,” he told UPROXX. “It’s a game that 'til this day I miss, and I miss playing. It’s not that I want to go play again, but I miss so many aspects of it; being around the team, being around the coaching, that calm before the storm.”

Nnamdi first caught the acting bug all the way back in 2008, when he landed the role of “Party Guest with Wife” on the CW sitcom The Game. (Remember: There are no small roles, only small actors!!) He then caught the eye of director Peter Berg on the set of a Nike commercial shoot in 2009, who was impressed by the athlete’s ease in front of the camera. From there, Nnamdi went on to play small roles in Friday Night Lights, Leverage, and Cubed before making his on-screen feature film debut in 2012’s Fire with Fire.

Since retiring from the NFL, Nnamdi has fully committed himself to his acting career with starring roles in the films Crown Heights, Sylvie’s Love, The Good Nurse, The Knife, and—most recently—The Body. He’s also served as a producer on several of those projects as well.

Despite all his success, Nnamdi still remains as modest as ever, avoiding social media and press to instead focus on his craft. “I’m really bad at self-promotion. I know that about myself,” he previously explained to Bleacher Report back in 2017. “People told me all of the time, ‘You could be such a big star if you just talked about yourself more,’ but I’m not good at that. It’s always been about team.”

During a 2018 interview with Today, Kerry corrected a host when they alluded to her two children, saying, “I’m a mother of three." The reason: Nnamdi has a now-teenage daughter from another relationship, who Kerry has helped raise alongside her two biological children. And just like with Isabelle and Caleb, the couple keeps their eldest far from the paparazzi, so very little is known about her.

As Kerry joked to People, “I have moments where I take pictures or videos of one of my three amazing kids and I wanna post it online, and I tend to just send it to my parents or my shrink instead.”

Philanthropy is important to Nnamdi, which is why he created the Asomugha Foundation in 2005 at the very beginning of his career.

The foundation has two main programs: Orphans and Widows in Need (which provides food, shelter, medicine, vocational training, literacy efforts, and scholarships to widows and orphans in Nigeria) and Asomugha College Tour for Scholars (which takes selected students from Bay Area and Los Angeles area high schools on college tours across the country).

Though Kerry and Nnamdi are both actors and producers, it isn’t often that they work together on creative projects. Back in 2017, Nnamdi told Vanity Fair that it was possible he and his wife would co-produce a project at some point.

“We would produce,” he said. “We’d probably produce before acting, I would think.” The following year, his production company, iAm21 Entertainment, helped produce American Son, a Broadway play starring Kerry, and hopefully, that won’t be the last time the two are involved in the same project.

As far as sharing the screen goes, however, Nnamdi doesn’t think that will ever happen. As he told The New York Times, “I produced American Son, but as actors, there’s no plan as of now for that collaboration. We’re very supportive of each other’s journeys, but we’ve always been that way. We always want the best for each other in whatever we’re doing.”

Case in point? The couple’s *chef’s kiss* appearance at the 2025 Met Gala. Ahem:

Oh, and the duo’s 2025 Golden Globes looks:

And ofc, he was right by Kerry's side during her December 2024 Hollywood Walk of Fame ceremony, where she finally received her well-deserved star. How cute do they look with Kerry's parents??

TL;DR: Kerry and Nnamdi truly couldn’t be any cuter. Keep on being adorably low-key, you two.

#6958. A Menorca Farmhouse Finds Its Easy Elegance

Isabel López-Quesada honored the historical essence of a 19th-century Menorcan retreat while introducing vibrant colors and an elevated ease.

Above: In the living room, López-Quesada replicated the original ceiling beams and finished the walls in traditional Menorcan lime plaster. A sofa of her design is grouped with a 1960s Tito Agnoli rattan daybed, a Mexican palm leaf stool, and a custom cocktail table upholstered with a Moroccan rug.

For Spanish designer Isabel López-Quesada, summers tend to revolve around Biarritz, in southwestern France, where she has a home. Still, she always carves out time for Menorca, regarded as the most laid-back of Spain’s Balearic Islands. That vibe suits the ELLE Decor A-list designer, whose signature style is luxurious but unfussy, harmonizing old and new with breezy refinement.

Typically, López-Quesada goes there to relax and spend time with friends, but around five years ago she received a call from a Bilbao family who asked her to refresh their vacation home outside Mahón, Menorca’s capital. An old farm property, or finca, the five-bedroom residence required extensive updates. López-Quesada was steadfast in preserving such historical elements as a well and donkey stables, which she transformed into an indoor-outdoor bathroom with a patio and marble tub. “It was important to respect the style of Menorca,” she says. “We were very careful with the materials, so it all goes together and conveys the sentiment I want the house to have.”

López-Quesada founded her namesake studio as a precocious 20-year-old Madrileña 40 years ago. Initially inspired by her mother’s yen for decorating and the work of celebrated Spanish designers such as Paco Muñoz and Fernando Benjumea, López-Quesada garnered wide attention in the early 2000s, when she converted an old Madrid wax factory into an atelier and a luxurious home for her family, filled with antiques and contemporary pieces. Soon she had interest from clients all over the world. Today López-Quesada’s 12-person team includes one of her three adult children, daughter Isabel Llanza. Five years ago the pair co-founded the lifestyle brand Isita Home, selling products they make themselves or discover on their travels.

The Menorca project began by gutting the house’s interior and rebuilding it “in an old style,” López-Quesada says. Central to it all was adding custom windows and doors to each room so the island’s golden light could flood the spaces. Vaulted and beamed ceilings were left intact or were recreated. Reconfiguring the floor plan, López-Quesada eschewed a proper dining room in favor of an eat-in open kitchen. “Most of the time you are eating outside,” she says.

With Menorca’s unrelenting summer sun, bougainvillea-draped pergolas were a must for leisurely alfresco lunches and dinners. Swaths of white lantana and lily of the Nile border the terraces, while “the pool reflects the sky like a mirror and provides a refreshing feel,” López-Quesada says.

Her collaborator on the gardens was Spanish landscape architect Luis González-Camino. “My vision was to design a water-efficient Mediterranean garden, divided into distinct yet connected ‘garden rooms’ for different seasons and times of day,” he says. González-Camino preserved the property as a natural Mediterranean woodland, with wild olive trees providing cover.

Indoors, López-Quesada installed French terra-cotta floor tiles chosen for their aged look. The three fireplaces were all handcrafted in traditional Menorcan style—a little imperfect, painted white—and decorated with vintage tiles the designer keeps in her Madrid warehouse.

She offset the white ceilings and lime plaster walls with vibrant textiles and tilework in blues and greens inspired by Menorca’s native palette. An upstairs bedroom is anchored by an iron four-poster bed with a blue-and-white-striped canopy; its bath has a shower lined with lapis-colored zellige tiles. Doors, which across Menorca are customarily painted in bold shades, here are finished in verdant hues.

Custom cabinets are designed with whimsically patterned cutouts for airflow, since humidity is a major factor. “On the islands, if you close the cupboards completely, mushrooms will grow inside,” López-Quesada says.

Step Inside This Renovated Menorca Retreat

She arrayed the spaces with a lively mix of vintage and new furnishings, emphasizing organic materials: rattan chairs, bamboo shades, Moroccan rugs, lots of linen and cotton fabrics in spirited florals or stripes. “If I use a heavy piece, then the rest is lighter,” she says. The tableware maintains the blue and green theme, including pieces from Isita Home and hand-blown glassware from neighboring Mallorca.

In the end, the owners asked López-Quesada to redo their home in Spain’s Basque region as well. It’s one of several projects she currently has across the country, including reimagining a hotel in Barcelona, and she’s also designing homes in Paris, Shanghai, and the Dominican Republic. Many of her projects are with repeat clients. “That’s how it works,” the designer says. “It’s like a chain.”

This story originally appeared in the November 2026 issue of ELLE Decor.

Sarah Cristobal is a writer, editor, and creative consultant. She is currently editor-at-large for 10 Magazine USA, among other freelance projects.

#6946. Has Zegna Cracked the Code to Modern Tailoring?

With its latest jacket, the Falcon, the brand brings some sporty utility to its collection of mix-and-matchable clothing. Artistic director Alessandro Sartori explains how it’s all part of a bigger project to help men dress their best.

Pay attention to the world of menswear long enough, and you’ll notice that certain quandaries come up time and again. A favorite among traditionalists and avant-garde types alike is the fate of the suit. It’s been incorrectly declared dead enough times that the handwringing over its demise has largely shifted to questions of its evolving role in the modern guy’s wardrobe: Who wears a suit, and when, and why?

According to Alessandro Sartori, the artistic director of Zegna for a little more than a decade now, there’s another question we should all be asking ourselves: What exactly is a suit these days?

“Everything we do here is grounded in tailoring techniques and in tailoring fabrics,” Sartori tells me over Zoom from his home in Milan. “But I'm a big believer that a suit doesn't necessarily have to be a combination of the classic top and the classic bottom in the same fabric and color.” It can be comprised of those things, he stresses. But the “modern suit,” as he calls it, can also be an intentional pairing of a different kind of top—a chore coat, an overshirt—with a trouser with a different fabrication. The materials and colors should feel complementary and cohesive but needn’t be perfectly matched.

“This is not only a way to give a new life to suiting,” he explains, “but to dress men differently—and, at the same time, keep the quality, the craft, and the beautiful fit that a tailored piece has.”

The idea is to adapt classic techniques to the realities of getting dressed today. “Many fashion brands offer a nice quality, but is not the handmade quality that we do,” Sartori says. “And there are a lot of brands that are offering beautiful, beautiful quality, but they are quite classic.” Since he started in 2016, Sartori’s overarching project has been to populate the gulf between those two modes of production, making clothes that feel contemporary but are rooted in the top-tier fabrics and handwork that helped Zegna become the powerhouse of Italian fashion that it is today.

The latest addition to Sartori’s lineup is a new jacket, the Falcon. It pays homage to the sort of utilitarian jackets Zegna founder Ermenegildo Zegna might have worn while walking the grounds of what is now the Oasi Zegna nature reserve in northern Italy, at the base of the Biellese Alps. (According to company lore, Ermenegildo imagined those walks as being accompanied by the flight of a falcon.)

Sartori acknowledges that the use case, in 2026, might be a little different. “I don't think that people are necessarily using the jacket walking up in the mountains,” he says. The Falcon is more about embracing a “sporty-chic attitude,” whether you’re stomping around the country or the city, and working within the modular framework that the designer has created at Zegna. You can mix and match it with other pieces—both old and new—from the brand, and you can wear it as part of a “modern suit” or on its own.

The new design is cut from Zegna’s Oasi Cashmere for lightweight warmth, and Sartori calls it “a great addition to our vocabulary.” Buttoned patch pockets give you somewhere to stash your stuff, a bowling collar can be flipped up to shield against stiff winds, and an internal drawstring can be cinched at the midsection for a little extra protection against the cold. Ultimately, it’s a familiar-looking piece. But it’s exceptional in its construction and materials and its design—a streamlined take on the hunting jackets of yore—is as considered as how it’s made.

“Long-lasting life is a key factor for what we're doing, and I don't mean only quality-wise,” Sartori says. “I also mean design-wise.”

The designer, for his part, hopes that the Falcon—alongside other modern classics from the brand—will be the sort of thing that guys wear and reinterpret for years before passing it on to the next generation. And as the brand continues to grow and evolve alongside its customer base, one of his biggest aims is to ensure that each item he introduces remains relevant throughout its (ideally long) lifespan.

“It’s like writing a book, and every season I add a chapter,” he says. “Because the story is going on, is moving with our clients, our community of people, our brand, our family. So many layers are … I don’t want to say ready, but cooking. I see a bright future for the brand, following this principle of walking with our customers and building an indelible aesthetic. I like this approach.”

#6945. The Rise of the Maximum Visibility Bra

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The summer of 2014, I was a college student working at a small-town lingerie store that lined its walls exclusively with neutrals. Every day, I sold women flesh-toned bras—black if they were really feeling kinky—all while filling my own intimates drawer with the same styles. Undressing in front of a guy that same summer, he voiced genuine disbelief over my nude, industrial-strength strapless bra. He wasn’t so much confused over its gravity-defying function as the fact that it was there, at all.

The early 2010s set a nearly impossible goal for young women: naturally perky breasts without any apparent scaffolding. But that’s all changing. Pop princess Adela’s exposed bras have become as central to her identity as her electric pink hair. Once left to jiggle freely beneath semisheer cotton, Charli XCX’s chest is now more often displayed in intricate lingerie. Several months back, Saint Laurent’s Spring 2027 front row was littered with boudoir-inspired looks while Prada and Fendi literally sent bras-as-tops down the runway. Bras have come out of hiding.

Somewhere between the “free the nipple” movement in 2012 and the impending #MeToo revolution of 2017, boobs became less of a sexualized spectacle and more of a political battleground. Victoria’s Secret canceled its fashion show as basics-driven intimates brands like Cuup and Arq gained traction for destigmatizing natural shapes. Then, breasts bounced back into cultural focus. The shift might be credited to the climate—conservatism generally propels women to take shelter in traditional femininity, or it is thrust upon them—as well as the twin forces of Sydney Sweeney and semaglutide weight loss. Either way, women got thinner while their chests grew larger—reflecting a beauty standard we hadn’t seen in a generation. Implants entered a new era of demand and dialogue, as celebrities like Kylie Jenner and Megan Fox transparently joined the big-breasted brigade.

But boobs aren’t just bigger—they’re bolder. Gone are the days of basics and bralettes. It’s all about va-va-voom underwire, satin, and lace—even push-ups have returned to the conversation. Still, “there’s a difference between showing your bra and showing off your bra,” discerns Tara Ocello, director of brand marketing at intimates brand Skarlett Blue.

Last month, the recently revived Victoria’s Secret Fashion Show broadcast Jenner strutting down the runway, her 445-cc-moderate-profile silicones outfitted in a record-breaking $50 million bra. Bella Hadid, Candice Swanepoel, and Adriana Lima showed off the iconic mall brand’s finest embroidered balconettes, lacy push-ups, and satin plunge bras. Angels aside, the reintroduction of these runway collections is also indicative of a shift in consumer product demand—a spokesperson for Victoria’s Secret claims there’s “strong interest” in fashion-forward details like lace, satin, logo hardware, and the brand’s statement shine straps. The Very Sexy Push-Up Bra remains the number one of the three mainline bras in its “Very Sexy” category. “Today’s customer wants a bra that performs beautifully and deserves to be seen,” says a Victoria’s Secret spokesperson. “It’s expanded how customers engage with [our bras] as both foundation pieces and intentional styling elements.”

The luxury London retailer Agent Provocateur has been positioning its designs as statement pieces since it launched in the mid-’90s—the last time exposed bras enjoyed prevalence. Creative director Sarah Shotton noticed the trend bubbling several years back on the Glastonbury grounds and immediately returned to the design studio. “We predicted this and designed [for it] about a year and a half in advance. I am constantly thinking, could it be worn as an outerwear bra?” she says. “I think if customers are going to invest in it, they want it to work harder than just in the bedroom. Lingerie as outerwear is just getting bigger and bigger.”

Category transcension is, of course, good for business. Victoria’s Secret reported sales up 10 percent in its second quarter, and Agent Provocateur has also seen revenue growth this year. Eager to capitalize on the movement, contemporary brands like Lioness are now engineering built-in underwear to imitate the effect of an exposed bra. This week, Charlize Theron wore a similar style by Tom Ford to the Governors Awards.

I have, undoubtedly, been influenced. This year, I have spent more money at Victoria’s Secret than cumulatively on lingerie in the past decade—acquiring my own Very Sexy Push-Up, and a turquoise polka-dot number. These are bras I’ve never gravitated toward, let alone purchased, because they seemed like impractical additions to my wardrobe. I’ve had to ask myself: Can you wear leopard-print under a white tee? What if the lace breaches my neckline?

Somewhere between Erin Brockovich and Blake Lively’s character in The Town, an exposed bra—particularly a colorful one—became a media shorthand for unsophistication, and more often than not, sexual availability. In simpler terms: tacky. Now, tacky is trending. Along with diamanté-encrusted snapbacks and sky-high peep-toes, straps in dynamic hues are stretched across the décolleté of It girls en masse. Printed cups are peeking out of the necklines. Some fashionistas are forgoing the shirt altogether. “There's a real cultural shift from polished perfection toward something more expressive, unpredictable, and a little undone,” claims Ocello. Or, perhaps a little more human.

This certainly isn’t a trend for everyone. According to Shotton, Agent Provocateur’s biggest sellers are corsets—a style that was once also considered strictly lingerie, but has since sashayed into the light. One wonders if their popularity is a gateway to full bra-baring, especially as GLP drugs become more readily available, and women more comfortable in their bodies. But with a Blue Wave washing in Woke 3.0, how long do boobs—and their bold accessories—have left? In all seriousness, might we look back on this moment with the same disdain we once reflected on early-aughts raunch culture? Only social media will tell. In the meantime, I plan for my collection to keep growing.

#6927. Chanel Chance Eau Splendide Is My Favorite of All the Chance Perfumes. Here’s Why I Love It So Much

Allow me to make the case for wearing it year-round.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

My mom’s Chanel No.5, a fixture in our bathroom cabinet, was the first perfume I ever knew by name. It’s sophisticated and timeless, much like most of the other best-of-the-best Chanel fragrances that have been released since No.5’s 1921 debut. And while I have nothing but respect for the classics, I’ll admit that some of the brand’s more traditional floral fragrances aren’t necessarily what I reach for on a regular basis. I’m usually more interested in something a little fruitier, fresher, and more playful.

Enter Chanel Chance Eau Splendide, the purple-hued 2025 addition to the brand’s beloved Chance fragrance collection. With a sparkling raspberry opening, a heart of rose geranium, and a soft, musky base, it promises a bright, youthful take on the classic Chanel scent profile. And as someone who spends a considerable amount of time sniffing new perfumes for work, I know things are serious when they earn an immediate permanent spot on my vanity.

After attending the launch event for Eau Splendide last April, I didn’t intend to make it my new go-to... but that’s exactly what happened. I got an early sample, started wearing it, and simply noticed that I was reaching for it nearly everyday. I quietly fell in love with it as it sneakily became one of my signature scents. Now, after wearing it pretty consistently for a year and a half, here’s my honest review of Chanel Chance Eau Splendide Eau de Parfum, including what it actually smells like on skin, how it wears throughout the day, and whether it’s worth the luxury price tag. Ahead, everything to know before adding it to your fragrance collection.

First things first: Chanel Chance Eau Splendide is a fruity floral, with a much brighter opening than you might expect from a traditional Chanel perfume. I’m not exactly surprised that I love it so much, given that fruity florals make up about 90 percent of my perfume collection.

The fragrance features a raspberry top note that gives it a juicy, slightly tart fruity sweetness. But rather than leaning into the saccharine, sugary territory of some fruity perfumes, Eau Splendide then introduces rose geranium as a heart note, which adds a fresh, rosy, and surprising-but-subtle minty moment. To me, this is how you construct a perfect fruity floral: Before it gets too sweet (and for me, headache-y), some fresh floral notes balance everything out.

But that’s not all: As the fragrance dries down, you’ll experience base notes of iris, cedar, and white musk that make for a soft finish. To me, it results in a scent that feels elegant, feminine, and sophisticated—very Chanel—without being particularly heavy.

Splendide is a fresh and light scent, but don’t think that means it’s not staying put on your skin throughout the day. It usually lasts about four to six hours on me, so while it’s certainly not the longest-lasting fragrance I own, about four spritzes of it gets me through most of the day.

With so many great Chanel fragrances, how does this stand up against the rest? If I had to describe the overall vibe, I’d say that Chance Eau Splendide makes me feel effortlessly put together, but it also has a playful streak to it. I smell the raspberry most prominently on my skin, though, as it gets later in the day, I also notice the iris and musk. Even though I have dozens of fruity floral perfumes, it’s different than any of the others in my fragrance wardrobe.

The first time I spritzed it, I did wonder if it was going to be too sweet for me. Ultimately, I found that the raspberry and rose geranium mix well together and don’t feel too sugary whatsoever. I also love how youthful it is, especially compared to some of the other Chanel fragrances out there. It’s super flirty, vibrant, and fresh, making it the perfect companion for daytime meetings, date nights, and more—I even wore it to Coachella this year. All in all, the one word I’d use to describe it is approachable. Is it the most complex perfume I’ve ever sniffed? No. But the scent, the vibe, and the longevity make it perfect for anyone from a first-time Chanel fragrance wearer (I think it’s a great introduction to the brand) to a fruity floral fanatic. For me, it’s an everyday bottle that takes me from day to night all year long.

On that last point: I can see why someone might call it a spring or summer perfume, given the notes. But there’s no rule that says you have to put away your fruity florals when temperatures drop. This is the kind of fragrance that works whenever I’m craving something bright and fresh.

Okay, so is Chanel Chance Eau Splendide actually worth the splurge? It’ll definitely be a re-purchase for me, and not just because it comes in a pretty purple bottle.

Here’s my case for giving it a chance—yes, pun intended. The juicy raspberry opening is playful without veering into candy territory, while the other notes give it that effortlessly polished Chanel feel. It’s the kind of fruity floral that doesn’t need to be retired the second sweater weather arrives. I say we let this one live its best life all 12 months of the year.

Of course, Chanel prices are Chanel prices (even the smallest bottle is $115), so I’d recommend giving it a test spray before committing to a full bottle—especially if you typically gravitate toward super-sweet gourmands or bold, spicy fragrances. But that being said, I’ve been wearing it regularly for well over a year and I’m nowhere near the end of my bottle, so it’ll last you a while. For anyone who loves a fresh, feminine scent with a little personality, Eau Splendide is definitely worth a sniff. That said, since it’s such an easy-to-wear, clean-meets-powdery-meets-sweet scent, I’d consider it a pretty safe blind buy if that sounds like the type of perfume you generally gravitate toward.

I’ve worn this to weddings, concerts, dinners... You name it, you can wear this perfume there. She’s effortlessly versatile, isn’t overpowering, and isn’t a snob—even though she’s polished, there’s no intimidation factor, and she doesn’t take herself too seriously. She’s a must-have for anyone, whether you’re newly dabbling in fragrance or someone who already swears by a classic fruity floral. Consider this review my formal petition to add it to a list of all-time best year-round scents.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmo who covers all things beauty, including fragrance. For this story, she shared her experience wearing the Chanel Chance Eau Splendide Eau de Parfum, one of her go-to perfumes for year-round freshness.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmopolitan and was previously the beauty assistant at Good Housekeeping, Woman's Day, and Prevention.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Score 18% off Apple’s latest and greatest.

It’s no secret that Apple has been dominating tech headlines this year. Right on cue, Amazon’s Black Friday sale is already here, and with it comes with a record-low deal on the brand-new Apple Watch Series 12 and a sub-$200 price on the entry-level Apple Watch SE 3.

Below, we’re breaking down the differences between each model—and peeling back the curtain on our price-tracking prowess—so you’re all set to shop. After all, when Apple gadgets are this cheap, they tend to go like hotcakes, so don’t say we didn’t warn you when they inevitably sell out.

Commerce editor Luke Guillory argued that compared to other fitness trackers on the market, Apple was lagging behind with its lack of readiness score functionality—until now. The Apple Watch Series 12 marks the debut of the brand’s very own readiness score thanks to a revamped tracking system. Plus, the watch now measures your heart rate more frequently to deliver Heart Rate Variability (HRV) readings every five minutes. If you’re trying to pay closer attention to your health this year, it’s a smart buy, made sweeter when it’s on sale for Black Friday.

“It's an accurate and predictive workout tracker. It counts my steps and activity and connects to my phone to play music and receive messages and calls,” he says. “If you’re new to the Apple Watch and want to dip your toes, this is where I recommend you start.” It has the same sleep tracking features, like nightly sleep score and sleep apnea notifications, but lacks the ECG app or hypertension notifications of its newer siblings. It also has an 18-hour battery life so you’ll need to charge most nights.

This Black Friday discount brings the SE 3 back down to the record-low $199 we first saw during October Prime Day, making it by far the most affordable Apple Watch on the market.

Our philosophy when it comes to shopping for Apple tech during Black Friday? If you find the watch with the features you need at a price you can stomach, pick it up. It’s incredibly likely that the deals you see here are the best ones you’ll see for the rest of the year.

#6919. Before & After: Two Best Friends Turned a Stripped-Down Tudor Into a Colorful, Antique-Filled Dream Home

Designer Anne Hulcher Tollett understood her client’s signature aesthetic better than anyone.

Anne Hulcher Tollett can’t remember a life without her best friend, Van. They met in a playpen as toddlers and quickly became an integral part of each other's lives, with many childhood memories taking place in Van’s house growing up. Tollett has been with her best friend through every milestone, including as her maid of honor at her wedding to her husband, Sean, and when the couple put an offer on a suburbanized Tudor home in Richmond, Virginia.

The five-bedroom house had good bones and an intuitive flow, but it needed help getting some of its charm back. When Van and Sean bought it for their family of four (plus their dog, Jelly Bean, and a small flock of chickens), it was whitewashed and lacked architectural character. In short, it wasn’t up to par with Van’s eclectic style, according to Tollett, but as the principal designer of Hanover Avenue, she reassured her bestie that she could easily take it from suburban chic to warm, layered, and personality-filled without having to completely rework the interior.

“Van’s aesthetic is diametrically opposed to ‘simple’ or ‘builder-grade,’ and although this house had been meticulously renovated and maintained by the previous owners, some of the charm she loves had been removed,” says Tollett. The designer and her team focused on lighter renovations and cosmetic upgrades, walking her best friend through every decision, from wallpaper choices to reworking the staircase to highlight the amazing treasures Van has collected on her travels over the years.

Throughout the renovation, Tollett and Van leaned into their sisterly shorthand, resulting in a project with very few disruptions. “Our biggest challenge was creating unexpected quirky moments without taking down walls or undoing all of the thoughtful and well-built upgrades that came with the house,” Tollett explains. “We found places on tables, walls, and bookshelves to highlight everything Van and her family have collected over the years. It makes the house feel like theirs and not the product of some design firm.”

Walls were covered with grasscloth, patterns were layered on throw pillows and vintage rugs, and anything that wasn’t antique or pre-owned was chosen to add a touch of patina to the space, allowing Van’s collections to truly sing.

Once Tollett was finished with the renovation, the house told the story of their friendship and a life well lived. Because the designer knows Van like the back of her hand, she was able to create a retreat that feels like a true reflection of who she is and how her family lives without editing out any of Van’s eccentricities. “We understood her design sensibility and took something well-built but fairly plain and made it fun, pretty, and interesting at every turn, which is exactly how anyone would describe Van!” Tollett adds. Besides, who better to design your house than the person who knows you better than yourself?

FAST FACTSDesigner: Anne Hulcher Tollett of Hanover AvenueLocation: Richmond, VirginiaThe Space: A 4,600-square-foot Tudor home with five bedrooms, four full bathrooms, and two half-baths

The barrel back and turquoise chairs were finds that Tollett sourced for Van’s previous home, proving that timeless profiles and accessible sizes can work in many different spaces throughout their lifetimes.

“A room like this with a vaulted ceiling and white walls creates a wonderful backdrop for bold pops of color and patterns because it can visually hold its own against furnishings with personality,” she says. “Van had the beautiful sideboard, and we loved pairing it with the tattooed table.”

Possibly one of the most remarkable makeovers in the entire house, Tollett coated the entryway in deep blue paint, topping it off with darling wallpaper on the ceiling for added dimension. “This is a great example of how a can of paint, a roll of wallpaper, and some strategically placed treasures can transform a room,” she says.

Large-scale grasscloth wallpaper instantly gave the once-all-white box character and rhythm, setting a serene backdrop for the rest of the unique finds in this dining room. The chandelier hanging over the table had long been on Tollett’s list to use in a project, and Van’s home was the perfect spot.

“The dining room is right near the front door, so it’s hung to look like it's being blown by the entrance breeze—a true classic with a twist!” the designer says.

Above the antique bar (whose natural patina plays so well with the wallpaper), are two framed photos of puppets, which Van found during one of her globe-trotting trips. She carried them around in her backpack, and Tollett loved the story behind them so much that she had them framed. On the opposite wall, behind the piano, is a portrait of Van’s grandmother in a vibrant green dress.

The kitchen was well-maintained when Tollett got her hands on it, but it didn’t feel all that fun, and it needed more than simple cosmetic refreshes like other areas of the home.

“We removed the big-box island lighting and reworked the storage, hood, and casework on the range wall,” Tollett explains. “We did the same on the wall opposite the range,” she adds, which was in fantastic shape, so they kept it.

To give the cook space more character, the designer installed a custom Delftware tile backsplash. The island was also in good shape, so Tollett upgraded the hardware, faucet, face frames, and lighting above it, opting for three Pinch pendant lights which bring an interesting, natural texture to the space.

“Open concept kitchens are tricky because they are seen from every angle, so we knew we needed to improve the storage to hide clutter,” she adds. Opposite the range wall, Tollett removed the open shelves, creating closed casework instead and topping everything off with pretty hardware and colors that play well with the adjacent dining and living rooms.

Tollett knew she was going to wrap this powder room in a beautiful butterfly-and-bird wallpaper, so she custom-matched a paint to fully drench the room in a whimsical emerald green hue. They even had their wonderful wallpaper installers cut out a few extra birds and butterflies to extend the design onto the ceiling, creating a more immersive experience. “We wanted the powder room to pack an equal punch to the bold, blue entryway it connects to,” she explains.

One of the mantras Tollett and her team at Hanover Avenue follow is “wonderfully weird,” which the hand sculpture extending through the wall to hold the soap perfectly captures. “The space feels not only more fun but also much more personalized to our clients!”

During the renovation, Van’s mother passed away, so Tollett transformed this almost-forgotten area into an homage to the matriarch. “We reused her beautiful plate rack and hung all her flawless collections of plates on the walls,” the designer says, officially turning this room, which overlooks the vegetable gardens, into the “plate room.”

They papered the walls in warm neutral grasscloth and hung relaxed Roman shades to softly filter the natural light, furnishing the room with many of the fabulous pieces Van already owned (and loved). “This is a wonderful example of ‘reimagining what you have,’ and finding ways to remember the ones we love in our own rooms,” Tollett adds. “Van’s mom was the coolest!”

“Decades of sleepovers in Van’s childhood bedroom allowed me to execute exactly what she was hoping to achieve,” Tollett explains. Though there are many layers of pattern in this sitting area, the designer managed to prevent it from feeling busy by keeping the same serene color palette for most of the textiles, minus a few pops of red for a warm balance.

“This room is a grown-up translation of Van’s entire design sensibility that began when she was young and was nurtured in her adulthood during her travels,” Tollett adds.

On the other side of the room is a calming wash of warm white paint and natural textures, giving Van a visual place of rest ideal for sleeping. Tollett calls it a “well-collected, well-traveled, layered space that seems to have evolved all on its own—a sanctuary for Van and Sean,” which makes the designer especially happy since she was the maid of honor at the couple’s wedding.

The primary bath really only needed some cosmetic upgrades, but they did a lot of heavy lifting. Airy Roman shades soften the space while Van’s mother’s bench and one of her existing rugs give it personality. By opting for two warmer paint colors and swapping out key fixtures, such as the lights, hardware, and faucets, Tollett created a serene, sophisticated escape that looks completely new, all with the same bones.

Tollett tapped into her childhood memories with her best friend when designing this room. “During slumber parties, Van and I fell asleep across from each other in twin beds, so it felt fitting to create another bedroom that fosters the same kind of childhood chatter,” she says, adding that stepping inside is like stepping into a time machine going back to 1983.

Along with the beds, much of the look was directly inspired by Van’s childhood bedroom, the round green pillow on the bed having been plucked directly from it. The pre-existing Tudor windows were perfect for the vintage vibe.

Tucked away at the far end of the home is a spot connected to the sunroom lounge. Dubbed the “dayroom”—an informal spot dedicated to relaxation and reading—Tollett wanted it to feel cozy and as collected as the rest of the home but still a bit private. They washed the walls in a soft gray-green hue and did the same to the custom day bed, finishing off the room with antique bookcases in a complementary gray tone. It’s easy for small rooms like these to feel overcrowded, but the pale color palette takes away some visual weight.

As founder and principal designer of Hanover Avenue, Anne Hulcher Tollett is intimately involved in every project the Richmond-based design firm takes on, from high-end residential to boutique commercial. Tollett finds a thrill in uncovering her clients’ design styles and endless joy in creating their dream homes that reflect who they are.

#6913. 3 Gilded Age Estates You Can Buy Right Now

Envious of HBO’s The Gilded Age? Live out your Russell family dreams in one of these real-life period estates.

Above: Listed for $75 million, the Hillandale estate sits on 268 acres in Stamford, Connecticut.

HBO’s The Gilded Age is back for a fourth season, once again bringing viewers into the lavish homes where the Russell and van Rhijn families’ dramas unfold. Filmed at some of the era’s most impressive estates, the series serves up grand ballrooms, château-style manors, and sumptuous drapery in abundance. It’s enough to stir up a little real estate envy. Fortunately, three extraordinary Gilded Age homes are currently on the market, offering the chance to live out that fantasy in real life. Take a peek inside below.

Dating to the turn of the century, this lavish European-style estate—known as Hillandale—once belonged to the Sulzberger family, owners of The New York Times. Located along the Connecticut border with New York, the star of the 268-acre compound is an eight-bedroom mansion designed by architecture firm Hunt & Hunt that spans more than 15,000 square feet. Featuring elaborate millwork, moldings, plasterwork, and stained glass, the manor features plenty of amenities. Olympic-sized indoor pool? Check. Elevator? There are two. Grounds? They include formal gardens, four guest houses, a private lake, and even a hedge maze. The opulent spread is listed with Leslie McElwreath of Sotheby’s International Realty.

An Anglo-Italianate brownstone in New York’s Chelsea Historic District, the Van Ness House was built in the mid-19th century and was later enlarged to its present 7,800-square-foot size. Revamped by ELLE Decor A-List designer Miles Redd, the seven-level landmark pays homage to its elegant roots, with preserved plaster moldings, custom millwork, elegant wallcoverings, and heated oak floors in a retro chevron pattern. In the back, a landscaped private garden is paved in limestone and dotted with linden trees that turn golden in the fall. Atop the five-bedroom townhouse, which is listed with Nick Gavin and Amber Gregory of Compass, is a rooftop garden with views of Saint Peter’s Church.

Spanning 14,100 square feet, this Beaux-Arts mansion right off Fifth Avenue was constructed in 1902 by architect James R. Turner and William G. Killian. Its interiors, meanwhile, are by John Russell Pope, the genius behind the Jefferson Memorial and the National Gallery of Art. Clad in a medley of brick, limestone, and marble, the eight-bedroom house has retained its stately proportions throughout the years, including a formal dining room lined in carved wood panels. Also inside are a trio of staircases, an elevator servicing six of the seven floors, and two rooftop terraces. It’s listed for $35 million with Brian Meier and Amanda Barba of Berkshire Hathaway HomeServices.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6912. How a Connected Home Supports a Good Night’s Rest

How a wellness content creator uses Hubspace from The Home Depot to curate an environment for better sleep.

As a certified holistic health coach and wellness content creator, Carolina Salazar knows the importance of a peaceful wind-down routine before bed. She trusts Hubspace from The Home Depot that powers many of the smart devices in her home, to help ease her into a good night’s sleep.

Hubspace, The Home Depot’s exclusive smart home platform, powers more than 250 products across select private brands—like Hampton Bay, Husky, and Vissani—as well as national brand partners. It effortlessly connects everyday essentials, including smart security, indoor and outdoor lighting, appliances, switches, watering controls, and more.

Some of the Hubspace app’s most practical features help users personalize lighting and improve home security. Hubspace Connect, for example, lets you control compatible lighting from a single switch, while Vacation Mode is a security feature that turns lights and smart plugs on and off while you’re away. One of Salazar’s favorite features is Circadian Rhythm, which automatically transitions lights from cooler, whiter tones to warmer ones as the day goes on.

“I can set my lights to follow my circadian rhythm, so they shift automatically throughout the day,” says Salazar. “And when they start to turn warm in the evening, that’s my cue to slow it down.” That can mean cozying up with a book, brewing a cup of hot tea, or, in her case, lighting a candle at her bedside and journaling. When Salazar is finished putting her thoughts to paper, she uses the Hubspace app to adjust the color, temperature, and brightness of her lights, dimming them as she gets closer to sleep. This can even be programmed automatically in order to reduce screen time before bed.

“Colder rooms bring better sleep, so I also have Hubspace turn on my AC at night,” she says.

The Hubspace app is free to download and easily syncs with your home’s smart devices. You can enable voice control for screen-free commands—also a plus if you’re trying to reduce your pre-sleep screen time. And the app runs on both WiFi and Bluetooth—so even if the WiFi is down, your nighttime routine can still run seamlessly.

#6911. The Best Holiday Palettes for Every Vibe (Including Your Own)

Minimalist, maximalist, or somewhere in between? Find your holiday makeup match at Ulta Beauty.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Are you a five-minute-face person, or does your beauty routine involve three eyeshadow brushes, a playlist, and absolutely no rushing? Whatever your holiday makeup personality, Ulta Beauty has a palette to match your energy. Think multitasking kits that streamline your routine, complexion powders that make your skin look lit from within, and eyeshadow collections for every party invitation. Whether you're loyal to your everyday aesthetic or never met a sparkly shade you didn't want to swatch, party season is the perfect excuse to switch things up.

Ahead, discover the best holiday makeup palettes and gift sets at Ulta Beauty, whether you're treating yourself or shopping for someone on your nice list.

A minimalist approach to holiday makeup requires multitasking formulas that cover multiple steps without a dozen different products. Look for all-in-one face and eye palettes packed with complementary colors, so you can get party ready without overthinking what goes with what.

Meet your new getting-ready shortcut: Morphe Sky's the Limit Face & Eye Palette. With eyeshadows, blush, cheek balms, and setting powder in coordinating rose and mauve tones, this all-in-one compact takes care of most of the color decisions for you. Sweep the pale pink matte shadow all over your lids, blend a little of the deeper brown into your eyelid crease for definition, and tap the champagne shade onto the center of the lid for a hint of sparkle. Finish with a swipe of rosy blush and a little cheek balm to add dewiness. Pair the palette with the Morphe Riches Come True 5-Piece Brush & Tool Set with four makeup brushes and a mini sponge, all tucked into a metallic pouch that's easy to throw into your overnight bag.

Full-glam beauty invites you to play with color, whether you want a sultry smoky eye or something softer. The Charlotte Tilbury Hollywood Instant Look in a Palette offers two distinct takes on holiday glam, depending on how much drama you're going for.

Feeling a little mysterious? The Dreamy, Bronzed Beauty version features darker eyeshadows, including an especially on-trend matte burgundy, paired with a warm, sun-kissed bronzer for a moodier take on party makeup. Prefer something more delicate? Pretty, Glowing Beauty leans into pale pink tones for an ethereal, barely-there effect. Whichever you choose, blend the deeper shadow into your crease and along your lash line for definition, then tap a touch of the sparkly option onto the center of your lids to catch the light. Complete the look with the included bronzer and blush (matte or shimmery, whatever your mood), plus finishing powder to help even out your complexion. Both palettes come in glittery packaging that makes them tempting to give as gifts. (Or, you know, not.)

And because nobody wants their smoky eye turning into a smudgy mess before midnight (or after!), finish with a spritz of L'Oréal Paris Infallible 3-Second Setting Mist Spray to help keep your handiwork in place while you dance the night away.

The secret to a glowing complexion is combining finishing powders, blushes, and highlighters that deliver a healthy sheen without piling on heavy layers. If your beauty goal is less disco ball, more just-got-back-from-a-tropical-vacation, the Hourglass Ambient Lighting Edit Unlocked Palette in Wish is right up your alley.

The limited-edition face palette contains six powders that work together to blur the appearance of imperfections and give skin a flattering, diffused finish. Unlike chunky glitter, the finely milled formulas reflect light evenly to make your complexion look naturally radiant. For a glowy face, the versatile finishing powder doubles as powder foundation. Sweep it across your face to start, then add blush for a pop of color, and concentrate highlighter on the high points of your cheeks for a little extra definition.

If you happen to be more of a "there's no such thing as too much sparkle" person, top the look off with Fenty Beauty Limited-Edition Midnight Diamonds Diamond Bomb All-Over Diamond Veil, which leaves a fairy-dusted effect on cheekbones, collarbones, and anywhere else you want to sparkle.

The ultimate gift for the beauty chameleon includes a mix of eyeshadows, mascaras, and lip colors to create entirely different moods. For the friend who changes her makeup as often as her outfit, Tarte You Know You Love Me Collector's Set has plenty to keep her entertained.

The collection includes two mini Amazonian Clay eyeshadow palettes—one with warm golden shades and the other with cool rosy neutrals—so she can go from bronzed and sultry to pretty in pink without buying another palette. She’ll also get two mascaras (Tartelette Tubing for length and Maneater for volume) plus two Maracuja Juicy lip products to give lips a glossy, plumped-up appearance.

Each eyeshadow palette comes with its own reusable wristlet clutch, giving you options when it comes to gifting. Split the set into two presents for your beauty-obsessed besties, or keep the whole collection for yourself. We won't tell.
