#6877. Quilted Liner Jackets Are the Swerve Your Wardrobe Needs. Here Are 10 Stellar Picks.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Layering is the name of the game in fall and winter. For the next few months, you are not a person; you’re a living, breathing Russian nesting doll. You are encased in strategic layers of t-shirts, sweaters, scarves, light jackets, and big jackets that you divest yourself of over the course of a day (or night). Like a knight donning his armor, you will spend this season dutifully putting on and taking off each of these individual pieces as you fight the seemingly endless battle against winter.

A few pieces are a given. A good tee certainly isn’t going anywhere, nor is a sweater. The specific kind of Big Coat you use varies depending on the guy and his style, but it’s nevertheless a guaranteed winter staple. Those middle layers can be a bit of a toss up, though, which is how you might encounter something like a quilted liner jacket.

Once upon a time, these padded jackets served as the interior protective shell worn under World War II era flight jackets before expanding into more mainstream outerwear. The quilted padding traps body heat, but doesn’t make the jacket so heavy that it just feels like another cumbersome layer. You can still wear it as a liner jacket—but lowkey, if it’s the right temperature, they also work pretty well on their own. So if you’re in the market for a new versatile transitional weather jacket, you’ve come to the right place.

If we had to pick one from this entire group, it would be this jacket from Buck Mason ... And it’s really not that close. Inspired by old-school mountaineering outfits, the nylon is woven in Japan, and the down fill has an exceptional warmth rating. Add a elastic cuffs and a fit that works layered underneath or overtop, it’s perfect.

Look, layering’s great and all—but no one talks about how annoying and awkward it is when you get hot enough to take off one layer, but then are stuck just kind of holding onto it until you get the chance to put it down or hang it up. That’s not a problem with this packable Paul Smith shirt jacket. Let it do its magic to keep you warm. When you’re ready to take it off, simply collapse it down, zip it up, and stuff it away into your pocket until you need it again.

Rains has a well-established reputation of balancing fashion and function with their outerwear, whether it’s their Esquire-editor favorite Fishtail Parka or this bomber liner jacket. Nothing beats a classic leather bomber...except maybe the rain (leather + rain = no bueno). That’s not an issue with Rains’ Banja bomber liner. You get that same edgy look, but with a slightly more practical construction. Plus there’s something sleekly minimal about the channel quilting. Layer it beneath your actual bomber or heavy coat of choice for optimal warmth all season long.

Look, we love a good Dickies moment. They make some of our favorite pants of all time, not least of which because they’re mad affordable—though that’s a pretty big element to it. So naturally, if someone comes up to us asking for a high-quality and stylish quilted liner jacket at well under $100, we’ve got one word for ya: Dickies.

Barbour’s been making sturdy outerwear that can withstand the rainy U.K. weather since the 1800s. And while they don’t strictly make a liner jacket, this lightweight—or “Flyweight,” as Barbour calls it—quilted jacket gets pretty close.

With a sleek, collarless design, this Fieldworth jacket can easily slide under any heavy coat. It falls more into “middleweight jacket” territory, meaning it’ll probably keep you toastier than some of the other options on this list—both on its own and when layered up.

If you’re looking for more of a typical fall/spring jacket, J.Crew’s PrimaLoft® jacket is where it’s at. The quintessentially Americana brand uses the down alternative to create a well-insulated transitional weather jacket you can layer with, or wear on its own.

Look, all these other brands and designers are great and all—but Alpha Industries is the real deal. No like seriously. When the company was first founded in Tennessee in the 1950s, it was as a clothing contractor for the United States Department of Defense. These were the guys making those sexy flight jackets long before Tom Cruise shrugged one on in Top Gun. Their Deco Liner nods to the OG liners of yore, featuring a slim, collarless design that will integrate with additional layers. Wear the jacket by itself when you start feeling warmer, and let the whole world see the Alpha embroidery on the back so everyone knows this is some top of line shit.

If you’re in the market for something lighter and less padded, opt for this cotton hunting jacket. The silhouette takes inspiration from vintage Czech military liners and traditional hunting gear to create a layer of outerwear breezy but still warm.

Get two jackets for the price of one with this reversible number from Cos. You get all the benefits of a quilted coat, with the versatile sleekness of a classic collarless jacket.

#6869. 27 Charming Christmas Towns That Look Like a Real-Life Holiday Movie

Think twinkling lights, towering trees, and Santa-themed bike rides.

The holiday season is in full swing, with many halls and houses already decked to impress in garland and fairy lights. But as you wrap gifts, prepare to host, and begin marathoning holiday movies, it can feel like the joyous season is coming to an end much too soon. We can't blame you if you're still wanting more from this merry time of year, and we've come up with a solution. There are plenty of beautiful Christmas towns across the country to fill your heart with peace, joy, and nostalgia—you just have to know where to look.

These towns are more than just beautifully decorated—they’re truly immersive, offering experiences from Santa-themed bike rides to orchestrated light shows and winter bar crawls. Below, we've rounded up the 27 most charming Christmas towns from all over the country, so no matter where you live, you'll be close enough to find a bit of extra cheer this year.

This picturesque mountain town serves as the perfect holiday backdrop for more reasons than just the ever-present blanket of fluffy white snow. Each year, the valley is transformed into a Christmas town with seasonal theatrical performances, Gunnison’s Night of Lights, a tree lighting ceremony, and even an ice sculpting event.

Ever since the formation of this city on Christmas Eve in 1741, Bethlehem, Pennsylvania, has been rooted in the holiday spirit. From Christmas markets to historical tours to horse-drawn carriage rides, there's no doubt this town won't feel magical. And make sure to stop by the historic Sayre Mansion, pictured here, to check out their elegant holiday decor.

Annapolis is known as one of the best cities during the holiday season thanks to its winter weather and historic downtown. If you're lucky enough to visit this Christmas town, make sure you spend time bargain shopping until midnight, checking out the spectacular light display in Sandy Point State Park, and viewing the Yacht Club Light Parade.

Chicago is exceptionally beautiful during the Christmas time. From the seemingly endless strings of lights to the Christkindlmarket, which is free to enter and so charming, the Windy City has many offerings during the holiday season, making it one of the best Christmas towns to visit. Plus, you don't want to miss ice skating or any Christmas-themed museum exhibits.

If you want to do something Christmas-y almost every single day of December, then Newport, Rhode Island, might just be the place to go. To create the perfect coastal Christmas, this town offers a month-long celebration that's been going strong for 54 years. From holiday bike rides to Medieval festivals to winter afternoon teas, there's so much to do in this Christmas town.

You'll feel as if you've traveled back to Charles Dickens's time when you walk through Franklin's historic Christmas festival. Characters from A Christmas Carol and Oliver Twist come to life throughout the weekend-long festivities. Roam the Victorian Village and try Victorian-era Christmas treats like sugar plums while listening to carolers.

A classic ski town, Park City offers Christmas markets, festive decorations, and a Christmas Eve parade lit by torches—plus spectacular views of the mountains are around every corner.

In the heart of Virginia horse country, you'll find one of the most festive Christmas towns. Cheer on the town's hunting hounds while sipping hard cider and wander the streets in search of vintage Christmas ornaments.

During Kennebunkport's annual Christmas Prelude, the town puts on a very New England celebration: You can watch Santa Claus arrive by lobster boat, go on seashore holiday trolley rides, and enjoy wine tastings and lobster bakes. Not to mention, the town is covered in Christmas lights and garlands that make it the perfect festive photo backdrop.

For a dreamy and unique experience, head to Santa Fe. One of the most anticipated celebrations is the Canyon Road Farolito Walk, which is when thousands of farolitos—luminaries made of paper bags, sand, and tea candles—cast a glow on the gardens and old buildings that line the road. You also can get your white Christmas fix by visiting Ski Santa Fe.

As one of the oldest towns in the country, Portsmouth is a charming New England destination that celebrates the holidays with a Vintage Christmas Celebration. Visit the historic village of Strawberry Banke or wander around the charming shopping districts in this historic Christmas town.

Get a Hallmark movie feel while experiencing a taste of Denmark in California's Solvang village, located in Santa Ynez Valley wine country. The destination hosts Julefest, a month-long holiday celebration with live entertainment, a parade, and a night market.

Koziar's Christmas Village in Bernville is one of the top attractions in Pennsylvania. With more than a million lights adorning buildings, barns, and the landscape—and a reflective lake that doubles the beauty—it's hard to beat the town's display.

This Bavarian-style village is charming any time of year! December festivities include a display of Santa costumes from all around the world, a gingerbread house competition, carolers, and plenty of photo ops.

Of course, New York City isn't a small Christmas town, but it is extra magical during the holidays. From the massive tree at Rockefeller Center to the various Christmas markets in the city parks with ice skating, shops, and more, you can't beat the Big Apple's holiday spirit.

Nicknamed the Lighted Poinsettia Capital of Texas, this town brings major holiday spirit every year. Visit during December when the lighted trail in Comanche Trail Park is aglow all month long. Check out Hotel Settles for its elegant Art Deco lodging.

During the holiday season, each home in Historic Williamsburg lights one candle, a tradition that dates back to the Revolutionary War era. The cobblestone streets and colonial holiday traditions such as caroling by torchlight make this destination a must, especially for history buffs.

This Christmas town stays decked out year-round, so if you're craving holiday spirit in July, you'll still find candy-cane posts, boughs of holly, and a Santa Claus house with live reindeer!

Christmas without the cold? We've got you covered! In addition to abundant sunshine and festive holiday decor, you'll also have plenty of history to explore in St. Augustine, America's oldest city.

Want to take a ride on the Polar Express? Visit Durango during the holidays. The historic mining town offers a Polar Express–inspired trip that your kiddos will flip over.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

Meghan Shouse is House Beautiful's Digital Editor, and she's been with the team since September 2023. On top of being the magazine's go-to Waco expert, Meghan also touches on quite a few other verticals in the digital space, like hot news content featuring your favorite HGTV stars, home design inspiration, and the latest interior TikTok trends. However, her favorite pieces are the ones that start conversations, such as stories about resale value and whether or not the Solo cup is chic. Before becoming a home writer, Meghan worked in the fashion industry for two years, writing for Harper's Bazaar after graduating from Iowa State University with a bachelor's degree in apparel merchandising. When she's not interviewing interior designers about home trends, you can find Meghan reading a fictional thriller with her cat in her lap, planning themed parties, or strolling through flea markets searching for chunky candle holders and vintage bookends.

#6866. Move Over, Balaclavas: The Snood Is Set to Be Winter's Biggest Fashion Trend

Alongside their cousin, the hooded scarf!

It was just a few years ago that the balaclava was everywhere. Fashion people referred to it as the winter headpiece you absolutely needed. But now, I’ve noticed a new star, and that’s the snood, alongside the hooded scarf. Most people over the years have blended these three headpieces, but I’m here to inform the masses: you’re probably wearing a snood or hooded scarf now, not a balaclava.

Balaclavas are a full-coverage piece of headgear that wraps around the entire head, neck, and face (leaving only the eyes, nose, or mouth exposed), whereas a snood is a continuous circular loop or tube of fabric designed primarily to insulate the neck and cover the top of the head.

Balaclavas tend to be closer to the face, similar in fit to a ski mask, while snoods are loose-fitting and have a sort of cowl design to them. According to the winter sports community, balaclavas are better for skiing and extreme cold, but for most of us, we’re just looking to warm up a little with our winter accessories.

If you’re ready to wear your own snood this winter season, I’ve gathered some stylish inspo below to help build out those mood boards.

This brown snood moment with a light beige trench coat is peak winter outfit. The headpiece pulls the whole cozy vibes together and gives ultimate cool girl.

As a Floridian girlie, shorts in the winter are my kind of speed. Throw on those uber warm tights I see scrolling down my TikTok ‘for you’ page and you’re in business. The hooded scarf just elevates the whole moment too and keeps your head warm, which is ultimately the goal.

Colorful snoods aren’t always top of the list, especially if you’re looking for a headpiece that can blend with all your outfits. But if you’re hoping to bring a bit more fun to your winter style, then grabbing a snood like this one above is the way to do it.

Monochromatic is always in, but getting super close to a full black look and throwing in a light-colored headpiece is my personal winter hack. It brightens up the whole outfit, but doesn’t a neutral color like the one above still keep the look uniform and stylish.

Aiyana Ishmael is the style editor at Cosmopolitan magazine. In her work, Aiyana focuses on the culture of fashion and how it intertwines and shapes the zeitgeist. She is an award-winning journalist from Miami, Florida, and a graduate of the historically Black university, Florida A&M. She is a 2024 Forbes 30 Under 30: Media honoree, and her debut romance novella PASSING GAME is set to release March of 2027 (831 Stories/Simon & Schuster).

#6864. This Wood Finish Was Once Seen as “Outdated.” But Designers Now Say It's Coming Back.

You might regret ripping out those kitchen cabinets.

For a period of time in the late 1980s to early 2000s, it seemed virtually impossible to enter a house and not come across honey oak. That bright, orange-toned wood finish covered kitchen cabinets, bathroom vanities, even baseboard trim and furniture around the house.

“You’d see it everywhere—cabinetry, trim, stair railings, and floors. It was the go-to wood tone for builders and homeowners alike,” Cathleen Gruver of Gruver Cooley tells House Beautiful. “But by the early 2000s, it started to fall out of favor as people shifted toward cooler, more neutral interiors.”

However, there’s been a noticeable shift in the way people are wanting their interiors to look, and warmer spaces are definitely back in style. With this evolution, can we expect to see a revival of honey oak, as well? We asked a handful of designers if they think this golden wood finish is back, and how they would style it for modern-day interiors.

Elizabeth Bennett and Mallory Robins, the founders of interior design studio Kobel + Co, believe honey oak is back for two reasons: The first is that warm tones throughout the entirety of the home are on the rise; the second is that honey oak has a lot of nostalgic associations for people who grew up in the '80s and '90s and are now homeowners.

“We're seeing a resurgence of styles from this era not only in homes, but in fashion as well, as we harken back to a pre-digital era,” Bennett explains.

“It’s not necessarily a full-blown comeback, but I’ve definitely noticed a soft return—particularly in cabinetry, millwork details, and furniture," Gruver says. “Clients aren’t always asking for 'honey oak' by name, but they are gravitating toward natural wood tones that offer warmth and character.”

Before you take a sledgehammer to those original honey oak cabinets, reconsider this tone by styling it in more modern ways. Keep reading to see fresh ways that designers are working with honey oak in their new projects.

As homeowners gravitate more toward lived-in, inviting interiors, honey oak is a wonderful way to add warmth. Leave it to the professionals to find stylish ways to make this once-outdated wood tone look cool again.

Plenty of designers will tell you to mix wood tones in your home to prevent it from feeling one-note, and the same is encouraged when honey oak makes a big presence. When designer Adina Hall took on the project above, there was too much honey oak in the space for her team to replace or refinish it all, so they embraced it instead.

“We developed a custom stain for the new wood floors, a whitewashed French oak that created a neutral canvas for the structure without competing with the existing honey tone,” Hall says. “We added custom cabinetry finished in a taupe-tone ash veneer, again a neutral tone, and we leaned into crisp whites and some cool tones like sage green and indigo blue as accents to create a balanced palette.”

Heavy amounts of warm woods can overwhelm the eye; it’s important to build a restrained palette and introduce interest with cooler tones, wood stains, sculptural furniture, and contrasting materials. “The key is to give the honey oak a contemporary context in order to make it feel like an intentional material that delivers a punch of warmth in an otherwise cool and architecturally bold yet restrained palette.”

Whether you’re adding in fresh honey oak or you’re trying to work around what’s already in your home, one of the best ways to ensure it feels modern and not like a ‘90s Tuscan-inspired home is choosing an earthy color palette. Muted greens, warm whites, greiges, taupe, light blues—these are all beautiful hues that won’t add even more orange-ish warmth to your space.

“Instead of making honey oak the focus of the room, I let the beauty of the wood shine through the use of clean lines, black matte touches, natural stones, and modern lighting,” designer Nancy Dow of Sierra Living Concepts says. She’s fond of using this wood, and uses these shades to create a modern and fresh appearance.

Rather than “wood drenching” a home in a single tone (as was often seen in the early aughts), Robins explains that current homeowners are exercising more restraint with their finishes. “Rather than the primary finish for a house on trim, floors, and cabinetry, it's being used in more subtle ways—a cased good piece or a kitchen island alone,” she says.

One of the reasons honey oak fell out of style is because it is a very warm tone, and in the world of “millennial gray,” an orange-tinted kitchen or bathroom isn’t very appealing. However, designer Marli Jones of Rebel House design studio believes this can be fixed simply by creating a contrasting palette.

In the kitchen space above, Jones used only honey oak cabinetry in the scullery to contrast the light, bright main kitchen. “It adds a coziness to the scullery and envelops the space in warm, natural tones,” she says. “That same honey color travels across the countertop stone and into the tile backsplash.” The two rooms are connected by the wall of glass and steel, but the change in materiality from the white cabinets to the honey oak creates a dynamic transition. “Warm wood always adds a grounding layer.”

When adding a honey oak piece into your home, do so intentionally. Opt for a “stunning built-in, a custom island, or as an accent detail that brings texture and warmth,” Gruver says. To emphasize the styling, pair the honey oak piece with contemporary hardware or painted elements to help it feel fresh and curated.

While honey oak can work in any interior aesthetic, there are a few styles it looks best in, according to Gruver: updated farmhouse and cottagecore. Basically, “any space that leans into character, craft, and comfort,” she explains. However, she's also seen the tone used successfully in more modern, organic spaces where there's a large emphasis on natural materials and textures. It all depends on how it's balanced, which holds true for any wood tone. She adds, “When styled right, honey oak can absolutely feel current again.”

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6863. Jennifer Lawrence Breaks Her Silence on Viral Connor Wood Smoothie Comment—and Corrects a Specific Detail

Might need to slightly edit my new favorite vocal stim.

“I saw you on 8th Avenue drinking a smoothie, and you paid me no mind.” Jennifer Lawrence typed these words on her keyboard, and they have lived rent-free in Connor Wood’s mind ever since. The viral moment even landed the Dancing With the Stars finalist a Smoothie King collab and had him pulling up to rehearsals in this iconic tee:

But the internet’s new favorite vocal stim might need a slight edit since J. Law herself publicly addressed it for the first time—and made a very specific correction.

#6862. We Really Need to Talk About the MobLand Season 2 Finale

A certain character’s fate hangs in the balance.

Nothing is simple in MobLand. Two seasons in, the plot has racked up more lines than Eddie put up his nose the night he stabbed Tommy Stevenson in season 1. Actually, that’s not strictly true. One thing is simple in MobLand: Eddie. He is a simpleton of truly Homeric (as in Simpson) proportions: a smarmy, sneering, simpering Freudian mess, channelled with gimlet-eyed precision by Anson Boon. All he wants is his God-given right to be respected.

Trouble is, when you’re an anti-Midas who turns everything he touches to shit, respect is a dish that never leaves the kitchen. Eddie is, in Kevin’s words in season 1, “a knifey little twerp.” That stabbing set the whole bloody saga in motion, so it’s only right that two seasons later we end with Eddie the Incompetent. Full circle, then, like a roundabout made of corpses.

MobLand opened with Eddie turning a stabbing into a gang war, so it seems fitting that we end with Eddie turning an assassination into an own goal. But we’ll get to that.

It seems Conrad’s chickens are coming home to roost, and his marbles are jangling around his head like loose change. We open the episode with his card being declined at a petrol station. In frustration, he shoots the till boy dead. But he knows what it means.

“We’re getting lifted,” he tells Maeve in the car. Police are raiding the entire business: bank accounts are frozen, warrants are out, and Tattersall is telling reporters the Harrigans have links to terror groups across the globe. The family convenes at a dingy old safe house out of town.

“We’re terrorists,” says Seraphina. “We’re looking at life in Guantánamo. It’s now or never.”

Her plan has been bubbling for a while. She calls it Harrigan 2.0: going global.

Remember the diplomatic suitcase of diamonds Seraphina collected from her billionaire stepdad Charlton in the last episode? It was meant to fund her and Bella’s takeover of the Harrigan operation. Now, it seems, Harry has it. He takes her aside to let her know he knows. “How did you know?” she asks, incredulous.

Then he says the three words he says more than any others in MobLand: “It’s my job.”

Inside the safe house, the family are all there, and everyone has a plan to pitch. Kevin wants to crawl back to Kat and hand over Conrad, Maeve, and Eddie. “Clean the house,” he growls to Harry, out of earshot of the others.

Conrad, meanwhile, isn’t sure what to do. “What are our options?” he asks Harry.

“We don’t have any,” comes the terse reply. “Either we run seven ways and it’s every man and woman for themselves, or we go back to Kat and try to strike some kind of deal.”

Kevin proposes offering her 50 percent and first working out who’s a liability. “And right now I’m seeing a lot of liabilities. Right, Harry?”

Bella has had it up to ’ere with Kevin’s pusillanimity. “Oh, you nearly got to the end of a sentence without saying ‘Harry,’” she taunts. “I know he saved you from a bumming once but… Jesus.”

Kevin pulls a pistol and fires, missing her ear by millimeters. Conrad draws. “Put the gun down, boy,” he hisses. Bella steps forward. “Go on then, big boy.”

You could cut the tension with piano wire.

“Fuck this, I’m getting us out of this mess,” says Seraphina, defusing the standoff by fetching a fistful of diamonds from the car. “Fifteen Type IIa brilliants. North of ten million euros,” she tells Conrad, placing them in his hand.

The plan: offer Kat a cut of the empire. “This time tomorrow we’re back in business.”

Remember that glorious, long-winded speech Conrad gave in Spain about the good old days, how he came from nothing, and how Maeve has been his compass through the carnage of his life? “We were born outlaws and we’ll die outlaws,” he bellowed.

Well, now he knows the jig is up. Looking balefully at the floor, he grunts: “Do it.”

So how will Harry play this? He slips out to his 4x4 and rings Kat. “At 5 p.m. I will meet your diplomatic courier. I will pass to said courier €10 million in untraceable diamonds. Upon authentication, I will give you the location of the Harrigan family.”

A double cross? It must be a ruse. He simply MUST have a plan… Then, hanging up, he mutters: “Fuck this, I’m done.”

He returns to the family and lies: “Kat will take the ten million and 50 percent.” Too easy by half.

As Frankie put it two episodes ago: “Is that it? Unless you’ve got some brilliant plans, that is actually a bit shit. What are you up to, Harry?”

We’re more than halfway through and haven’t heard a peep from Frankie, our laconic, crocodilian villain. Ah, but here he is, getting the news of Harry’s volte-face from Tattersall. “How do we know it’s not a bluff?”

“He’s not stupid,” says Tattersall. “He knows when the game’s up.”

“Well, you know me,” leers Frankie. “I can’t resist a gamble.” Guns are grabbed from car boots, and off he pootles with a gaggle of tooled-up goons to send the Harrigans back to Hell.

It’s all unfolding very quickly now, as denouements always should. Back at the safe house, Conrad is still seeing the dead, including the petrol station lad he just murdered. Maeve says he’s been saying their names in his sleep.

The old loon’s marbles are now departing his head like passengers from a cancelled flight. But there’s one thing that’ll straighten any man out: a black pearl of Maeve wisdom. “We’ve been stuck up tighter holes than Kat McAllister’s,” she reassures him. “But when you’re stuck up someone’s hole, there’s at least two ways out.”

Quite so. And who better to lead the way than Harry? As ever, he picks the exit that leads straight back to where he came in. Unbeknownst to us, he has gone up Kat’s backside, rummaged about, and come out the other end holding her entire career. The “betrayal” was a ruse from the start.

So when Frankie’s goons arrive armed to the teeth, it’s a trap: flash grenades, smoke grenades, poison gas, and Zosia on the exit with a machine gun for anyone daft enough to run. “Oh, Harry,” concedes Frankie, before a mighty gunfight erupts and Harry tears through the house like Keyser Söze in a gas mask. “So this isn’t the beginning of a beautiful friendship,” smiles Frankie, choking in the corner. Harry shoots him in the knee.

Then Harry rings Kat. Those “untraceable” diamonds? Very traceable indeed, straight to a terrorist organization. He has bribed a low-ranking diplomatic courier to deliver them along with a “little present, just for you.”

She opens the box, ashen-faced. It’s Donnie’s blue and rotting head. “This one you might find harder to justify,” he tells her.

“My family are gonna hunt you down and feed you your balls,” she spits.

“Probably,” says Harry. “But not before they feed you yours.”

Checkmate, Kat. The black queen has no more squares to go.

But wait … Harry can offer her one square. “I can fix this,” he says.

Here’s what she’s going to do. She’ll resign. She’ll kill the police investigation. The Harrigans’ accounts will reopen and they’ll come off the terror list.

Cut to a London corridor of power, where a faceless civil servant informs DS Kavanagh that “continuation of your terrorism financing line will interfere with an active joint intelligence operation between British and American security services. You will stand down at the close of business.”

And so, full circle. Maeve is electrocuting Frankie’s balls with a car battery. Kevin is building bridges with Bella. Conrad is going madder by the day. And Eddie?

The Semtex he blackmailed Paul for was meant for Harry, at Maeve’s behest. In the hands of any other Harrigan, this would be a doddle. But not Eddie. Not the knifey little twerp, a man who could trip over his own foreskin in an empty car park.

And he does. But first, the hints. Something strange has been going on with Jan all episode: the soppy Damien Rice soundtrack, the wistful dream sequences of the good times she shared with Harry. Her Harry. The one she fell for in the first place. She seems to have forgiven him. They’re even taking a family holiday.

But there’s one last stop before they go: Harrigan HQ, where Eddie is lying in wait. While Harry negotiates the terms of the holiday with Conrad, Gina and Jan stroll over to the lake. It’s Eddie’s moment, his chance to shine. He plants the Semtex in their car, hides around the corner, arms the bomb, finger hovering over the detonator … then a phone call.

By Finn McCool’s cobblers, it’s Kelly “Mad Dog” O’Connor, the lunatic son of the Medusa, whom Maeve sent Eddie to kill. “I’m coming for you,” growls the voice. “The Mad Dog is coming.”

Gina, Jan and Harry return to the car. Only Jan gets in. In his quivering, fearful state, Eddie trips and drops the detonator.

That’s Jan up in smoke, along with Harry’s plans for a quiet life. It may spell the end of Jan and all her problems, but it’s only the beginning of Eddie’s. He now has Mad Dog O’Connor to answer to, and Harry.

Then the credits roll, and what a gloriously cruel place to leave it.

The only other loose end is Frankie. We’re left to assume he met a long, painful death at the business end of Maeve’s car battery, but we never see a body. I truly hope he does a Harry and wriggles out of one last tight hole—Maeve’s hole—with one good knee and a long memory.

Season 3 can’t come soon enough. When Harry works out whose finger was on the detonator, Eddie’ll wish a knifey little twerp is all he could be.

#6861. Why Men Are Posting Thirst Traps in Red Light Face Masks

Gonzalo Fernández Escribano knew all about the benefits of red-light therapy for his skin. Fewer wrinkles, a brighter complexion, minimizing acne—he’d experienced it all firsthand when he tried out LED masks during his facials. In March 2025, he purchased one for at-home use. Little did he know it would come with an extra benefit as a provocative prop for a sexy photo.

"I use my red-light mask right before bed, so I’m already shirtless,” says Escribano. “Let’s just say the sexy-looking picture is easily convenient to take.”

Escribano is one of many men online who’ve embraced the red-light thirst trap. More and more, image-conscious guys with followings built around some combination of beauty, fitness, wellness, travel, are pairing their red-light routines with the occasional shirtless selfie. The trend is especially prevalent in gay-male internet culture, where faceless torsos shots, sometimes featuring beefy chests and six-pack abs, have been part of the visual language of dating apps and social media for years. A shirtless red-light-mask pic is a variation on the theme: The body and the anonymity are still there—only now there’s a skincare routine attached.

Jayson Stacy, a celebrity groomer, noticed his friend wearing an LED in his Instagram profile picture three years ago. At the same time, Stacy had recently gotten a mask himself and decided to follow suit, posting a photo of his own after Mardi Gras under the guise of some much needed recovery.

“It’s almost like a sexy Halloween costume, but you don’t have to wait until October,” Stacy says. Escribano agrees, saying it makes him look like a superhero. Strong shadows, glowing skin, and a Sith-like red glow mix together in a thirsty trifecta meant to slow someone’s scrolling.

For Stacy, red-light masks strike the perfect balance between wellness and plausible deniability. “You’re supposed to use the mask after you wash your face, so most are fresh out of the shower before using,” he says. “It’s like, ‘Oh, look at my morning routine,’ which gives the perfect excuse to be mostly unclothed. Then you have the mask on, you feel like someone can’t see your face, so it can feel like a layer of protection.” In other words, the skin care routine becomes the reason for the thirst trap, even though it’s only half the motivation.

Red-light therapy is a noninvasive treatment that uses low-level LED light to stimulate cellular activity and boost collagen production. The technology has become especially prominent over the last few years thanks to its ability to reduce inflammation, soften fine lines, and promote skin and muscle recovery and hair growth. (That is, as long as you are willing to invest in high-quality equipment and use it every single day).

Celebrities have been documenting their LED routines for years, from January Jones to Kim Kardashian. But those posts were mostly framed around the masks’ intended purpose: improving your skin. Somewhere along the way, though, a certain breed of extremely online men—trainers, models, artists, content creators, and guys who simply know their angles—found a second purpose for the masks: as an accessory for a hot photo. The masks are turning up in bathroom mirror pics, bedroom shots, and buried several slides deep in otherwise unrelated photo dumps, often without any mention of skin care at all. And judging by the comments—fire emojis, heart eyes, and the occasional “yummy”—their followers seem to be noticing something other than the mask.

The thirst traps are also surfacing on social media at a time when curiosity around red-light therapy is booming. A 2025 study analyzing Google Trends data found that search interest across red-light-therapy-related terms increased 118% after February 2024. And by 2025, Google reported that searches for “red light therapy” had reached an all-time high. As these futuristic devices have rapidly flooded our feeds, maybe it was only a matter of time before they made their way out of the skin care selfie and into something a little thirstier.

In some ways these images are being ushered into well-trodden territory. In 2021, TikTok’s viral Silhouette Challenge had users transform themselves into barely distinguishable, body-forward silhouettes under a red filter, turning the color itself into a visual shorthand for sex appeal. Meanwhile, for gay men, shirtless photos are hardly a novelty, especially on dating apps like Grindr and Sniffies, where headless torsos are practically part of the experience. “The headless torsos definitely all blend together at this point,” says John Arthur Hill, who compares swiping through hookup apps to looking into “a lukewarm batch of Chili’s skillet queso” and trying to figure out what’s cheese, meat, or a bell pepper.

And even when the most impressive six-pack can get lost in an endless doom scroll, red light can add just enough intrigue to get someone to look twice. For Adam Pent, an OnlyFans creator who’s no stranger to posting thirst traps himself, that curiosity is part of the appeal. “It [a red-light-mask thirst trap] is definitely more interesting because I’d be curious about who’s behind the mask,” he says. To Pent, there’s something creative about the whole thing. “I guess it makes the photo a bit more artful too? ‘Art thot’ vibes I guess, unless it looks like sponsored content.” It also makes him want to shop. “I’m less focused on the body and more like, ‘Should I just get one already?’”

The red-light thirst trap also holds a certain mysterious appeal. “I’ve been posting ‘masked’ selfies for quite some time because I think it’s a really hot aesthetic and such a cool fantasy,” Michael Anthony, a celebrity makeup artist, says. For him, it’s not just about looking good in the photos. “The fantasy of anonymity and pleasure, while doing something for yourself that will make you look and feel better—it’s all so sexy.”

An LED face mask is a luxurious way to get an at-home glow, and Shark's is one of our favorites. It helps to fight acne and fine lines, and it can help depuff around the eyes for those long nights.

Tattoo artist Alvin Archibald sees a similar appeal. “There’s a subtle tension between vulnerability and control that makes it interesting,” he says. “The mask takes away your facial expressions, so the body becomes the focus. It creates this interesting dynamic where you’re both exposed and concealed at the same time. There’s something almost intimate about it, like you’re getting ready for an audience without necessarily admitting it.”

#6859. James Bond’s Favorite Luggage Brand Has Broken Its Own Rules

Globe-Trotter has spent more than a century perfecting its signature suitcase. Now it's trying something different.

James Bond has always travelled in style. And while Daniel Craig's 007 was most immediately associated with his Aston Martins, Omega watches, and Tom Ford suits, his luggage of choice was Globe-Trotter, the Hertfordshire-based manufacturer whose cases have also found favor with everyone from Queen Elizabeth II to Kate Moss.

Now the company has done something completely left-field. After 129 years of making essentially the same kind of suitcase, it has changed the formula.

The new GT897 is Globe-Trotter's lightest case yet, and a significant departure from the design that made the brand famous. Out go the traditional rigid frame and locks, replaced by a zip-around opening and softer, more streamlined silhouette.

That said, some familiar details keep it firmly on-brand: the vulcanized fiberboard, leather trim, nickel hardware, and those trademark exposed rivets. And the cases are still handcrafted in Hertfordshire.

Currently available in two color combinations—bland-and-black and green-and-brown—the GT897 is priced at $1,395. A black-and-oxblood version follows in February.

It's an interesting move for a company whose USP has always rested somewhat on its refusal to change. Whether the new James Bond will follow Craig's lead remains to be seen. But at least there's now a new option for packing his tuxedo.

#6858. Forget Stainless Steel Appliances: This Designer-Approved Style Is the New Kitchen Status Symbol

Turn your hardest-working appliance into a design moment.

The kitchen has evolved from a hardworking cook space into a multifunctional room for lounging, gathering, and entertaining. So naturally, appliances are changing, too: They need to be as beautiful as they are functional.

Many associate appliances with stainless steel—it has been the go-to choice in kitchens for decades. The material even took center stage in our 1939 Washable House, where it made classic worktables and countertops easier to clean. But today’s design is making room for more colorful choices.

The kitchen designers behind our 2026 Whole Home in Charleston embraced that shift, showing how the room’s most essential appliance—the range—can balance utility with style.

For the kitchen’s centerpiece, designer Susan Ford of Bennett Hofford chose a La Cornue CornuFé range in Quintessential Teal. White-veined marble, creamy cabinetry, and golden hardware frame the deep blue hue, which continues across the lower cabinets to anchor the design.

The color also plays beautifully against the plush terra-cotta stools lining the island. Designer Chloe Legras of Boxwood Avenue, who designed the adjacent great room, selected the stools and other kitchen accents.

Beyond its color, the range features millwork in mixed metals, giving the traditional detail a futuristic edge and adding character to the small but bespoke space. A Parisian-style range hood with gold details completes the look.

If you’re looking to elevate your kitchen, take a cue from our Whole Home. This design is bold enough to impress the design-obsessed and practical enough for the bustle of everyday life.

#6857. These Evergreen Perfumes Are Perfect for the Holidays (And Won’t Make You Smell Like a Candle)

Turns out there are a million ways to wear pine, balsam, and juniper notes.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

As much as I can appreciate a good gourmand or fruity floral, I am a spicy, woody scent girl through and through. And when the wintertime hits, there’s nothing better than the fresh fragrance of coniferous evergreens: pine, spruce, juniper, cypress, cedar, hemlock... if a plant is spiky and verdant, I want to smell it. Until recently, these notes were something I associated with candles—lovely to burn while I was watching a movie by the fire, not for spritzing onto myself during the day.

But, in the past few years, a veritable sleigh-load of evergreen-forward perfumes have launched, and I’ve deeply enjoyed almost every one I’ve sniffed. So, in the spirit of the holiday season, I’ve chatted with some experts about why this particular subcategory of woody fragrance seems to be so popular now and what notes pair best with evergreen for a wearable scent, plus rounded up a few of our perfumes with notes like pine, fir, cypress, spruce, and juniper that are perfect for this most festive time of year.

This rise in evergreen notes aligns quite well with what’s going on in the rest of the perfume world. “When it comes to perfume, this year has been all about touching grass, getting back to nature and living IRL,” says Steven Gontarski, senior manager of brand and public relations at retailer Luckyscent and host of the It’s Just Perfume podcast. “These scents remind us that we're actually living beings with a relationship to the natural world and not just disembodied fingers scrolling on endless screens.”

While we saw nature-inspired perfumes that emulate the sun, juicy berries, even the stinkiest flower in the world launch this fall, so it make sense that the plants most associated with winter are getting their time in the scent spotlight right now. There’s also something inherently cozy about pine.

“Evergreen is what comfort smells like when it isn’t dessert,” says Jeniece Trizzino, vice president of innovation and physical product at fragrance companies Scentbird and Drift. Sweet gourmands have held on to the “most comforting scent category” for a while, but Trizzino agrees that people are seeking a connection to nature now.

“Old trees, forest floor, damp earth: These scents give people a way to reset,” she says.

Evergreen notes are fairly versatile, though they tend to find their way into scents that lean, for lack of a better word, unisex rather than what we think of as traditionally feminine fragrances.

Gontarski loves notes of evergreen paired with incense, which adds a smokiness to the scent. He’s a fan of bright citrus notes too, saying they create “a perfect counterpoint” to resinous notes like pine.

“The best pairings put evergreen somewhere real,” Trizzino says—in other words, these woodsy notes pair best with other scents you can find in nature. She loves them with herbs like mint, basil, or eucalyptus (a different type of evergreen, BTW) and damp notes like moss, wet stone, or ocean air. If you’re still seeking the warmth that’s so common with winter gourmands, Trizzino says evergreen also pairs well with amber, musk, tobacco, and whiskey notes.

Kicking off this list with what I feel comfortable calling the most-likely-to-please-everyone evergreen scent. Yes, the juniper and cedar make for an undoubtedly pine-y fragrance, but it’s paired with warm, creamy notes of cardamom, suede, bourbon, and vanilla that almost bring it into gourmand territory.

This year, Nest turned its best-selling Holiday candle into a perfume you can wear. I’ll admit, I was skeptical that it would turn out well, but the brand really nailed it: The scent is much juicier than the candle, thanks to grapefruit and cranberry, but still has that familiar balsam note.

If you like a juicier scent story, surprise! There’s even an evergreen fragrance for you. This one goes dual meaning on the name “pine,” with notes of pineapple and pine tree, plus some florals and citrus. The result is something that truly works year-round.

This scent smells way more expensive than its under-$50 price tag. Evergreen really leads the charge too: There’s both fir balsam and torched cedar, paired with patchouli and vanilla for a smoky, warm fragrance.

Come for the gorgeous bottle, stay for the invigorating blend of pine, bergamot, eucalyptus, and cypress. It’s not quite as good as a Mediterranean vacation, but it’ll do until I save up enough miles for another trip.

Both my partner and I love this savory gourmand scent. Coriander mixes with juniper for a bright, peppery opening, but it dries down to a musky, spicy, floral thanks to clove, clary sage, magnolia, and mace.

“My all-time favorite is Santal 33,” Trizzino says. “I like woods that are warm and a little creamy.” You’ve almost definitely smelled Santal 33 before, but you might not have realized it has an evergreen (cedar) at its heart.

This aquatic evergreen scent from trendy fragrance brand Noyz is similar in vibes to Santal 33, but a bit more subtle on the skin. It really does smell like the inside of a spa you’d be thrilled to visit on your shittiest of days.

This is a perfume that made me totally forget I’m not typically a fan of rose scents. D’Orsay pairs the flower with a cypress, vetiver, and patchouli base that all adds up to a fragrance that feels both familiar and brand new—like an acoustic cover of your favorite song from high school.

Another cult classic that’s packed with evergreen notes, Gypsy Water opens with juniper berries and bergamot, has pine needles and incense at its heart, then finishes off with a cozy amber-vanilla-sandalwood base.

Fresh, laundry day-inspired scents are another big trend right now. Indie brand Cayla Gray’s take is (as the name implies) quite floral, but there’s white cedar at the base that makes this fragrance smell like walking in the woods on a crisp winter morning.

Nightchild is one of Gontarski’s favorite examples of an evergreen scent. In it, “incense pairs beautifully with evergreen notes,” he says. “Perfumer Michael Nordstrand tells a story of moonlight penetrating a forest canopy with frankincense mimicking its silver light.”

Orphéon is a classic spicy evergreen scent that’s beloved by many already. Named for a Parisian jazz bar that was popular in the 1960s, the vibe is sexy, smoky, and a little bit mysterious. The note of ginger gives it a sweet-spicy touch that you won’t find in any of the other fragrances on this list.

This is a (literally) fresh take on evergreen, perfect for someone who’s seeking a scent that’s light and airy rather than dark and spicy. It’s packed full of familiar herbs; fir, balsam and cypress; plus fig and olive for a touch of savory gourmand goodness.

If you’re looking for a scent that combines both sea and forest, this unisex scent from Giardini di Toscana will suit you just fine. It’s a marine fragrance that blends a little bit of citrus, a little bit of cyprus, and some salty notes for a fragrance that smells like the color turquoise.

Another of Gontarski’s favorites, G.494 has lots of citrus. “Notes of bitter orange and mandarin create a perfect counterpoint to the balsamic resins and evergreen needles,” he says. “It’s clean and invigorating in an understated way.”

Kara McGrath is a writer and editor with over 17 years of experience covering the beauty industry, women’s health, and a wide range of other lifestyle and wellness topics. After most recently serving as content director at Allure (2019-2025) and executive editor at Bustle (2014-2019), she is now fully freelance. In addition to Cosmo, her writing has appeared in Allure, The New York Times, Marie Claire, Business of Fashion, Playboy, NH Magazine, and more. Kara lives in New Hampshire with her dog, four cats, and a rotating cast of foster animals. You can find more of her work at karamcgrath.com.

#6856. APC Just Dropped a Pair of Incredibly Clean Casios

We’re in a great era for affordable fashion-collab watches. Timex is leading the way: its link-ups with Noah, Todd Snyder, and J. Crew are masterclasses in adding personality to classic designs without going OTT. But don’t count Casio out of the race. The Japanese giant has just followed up its 2024 collab with APC. The pair are perfectly matched: Casio watches are known for being beautifully functional and understated—adjectives that fit APC’s spartan aesthetic too.

The first collaboration between the two brands saw them rework the hallowed A1000 digital-display watch. That release stripped back a lot of the original’s markings and added the APC logo on the dial. This time around, it's the AQ-240 getting a makeover. The case is squarer than the rectangular A1000, and while there’s a narrow window for a digital display, the watch maintains the traditional analog format with hands and hour markers. Here, you get the best of both worlds. The watch is sold out on APC's website but still available through Casio, so act fast if you want one. The silver variant is $110, and the gold goes for $135.

What about the APC-ness of it all? That part is the opposite of loud: as with the previous collab, there isn’t much giving away the brand's participation. We’ve got the APC logo on the dial and the buckle, plus the brand’s guitar-and-dagger insignia engraved out of sight on the caseback. Casio already offers the regular A1000 in a bunch of different colorways, but these two new ones are made specifically for the APC collab. The release includes a silver case with a dark-green dial and another in gold on gold. Both of the pieces have a brushed sun-ray effect on the dial. The vibe is refined and stripped back; this is a fashion watch that almost feels anti-fashion.

Maybe the best way to think about it is as the reverse side of the coin to the excellent new G-Shock 2500 Series. Both come from Casio, and both are minimal and functional. But the G-Shocks are big and bulky, with a design that seems to tip its hat to Patek Philippe’s Nautilus. These new ones are slim and nearly dressy. Put the two new releases together, and you’ve got pretty much all bases covered. And if those aforementioned Timex collabs felt a bit too classicist for you, then the Casio-APC partnership takes things in a more modern direction. Far from just being a cash grab, the “fashion watch” is now one of the best routes into affordable, on-point horology.

#6855. Hunter Schafer Masterfully Combines Decades of Prada Fashion

Something old, something new, lots of Prada, and something blue

The Blade Runner franchise imagines a bleak dystopian future, one where the world has been ravaged by nuclear war and humans are burdened with inequality and threatened by rogue robots. But if everyone dressed like Hunter Schafer, how bad could it really be?

While promoting her new miniseries, Blade Runner 2099—which serves as a sequel to the Blade Runner (1982) and Blade Runner 2049 (2017) movies—Schafer has been on a hot streak of futuristic, otherworldly looks.

So far, she’s worn everything from a neon-green Nihl hoodie covered in beaded florals to a double-layer Lii minidress that appeared to peel open from the back. Now, her latest ensemble plays with time in a different way—by looking space-age, sure, but also by uniting decades of fashion history.

At the Blade Runner 2099 photocall in London earlier today, Schafer stepped out in a pop-art inspired look that combined periods of Prada fashion.

Styled by Dara, Schafer embraced the exposed-underwear trend with a satin, cerulean bra, which was paired with a white wrap-around midi skirt with a patch of neon-green color. Both were pulled directly from the Prada Spring/Summer 2027 ready-to-wear collection by Miuccia Prada and Raf Simons.

While Schafer has long been a supporter of the storied Italian brand, here, she and Dara pulled out a reference for the true fans, layering on a transparent, PVC raincoat pulled from Prada’s Fall 2002 collection.

With its thick black trim, the vinyl outerwear could have been plucked straight from the pages of a comic book. And to match the edges, the coat was cinched with an inky leather belt, while Schafer wore a pair of pointy black heels down low. For a finishing touch, she rocked electric-blue eyeshadow that winked to the color of her bra.

Sometimes, it can be hard to feel optimistic about the future. But at least Schafer is here to imagine one that’s wonderfully colorful, fun, and stylish.

#6854. Who Will Be the New Bond Villain? We Nominate These 5 Actors.

Robert Pattinson has been confirmed as the new 007, but every Bond needs a worthy adversary.

As Alfred Hitchcock put it: “The better the villain, the better the picture.” And nowhere is this truer than for James Bond—a character whose movie is only as good as its baddie.

Jaws, Blofeld, Le Chiffre, Goldfinger—they are the daddies of all baddies; they put the “fun” in funeral, the “ooh-er” in evildoer. They all have a thing, too, because a good baddie always needs a thing. Jaws has the teeth, Le Chiffre the bleeding eye, Blofeld the needy cat, and Goldfinger, well, he’s a kinky old bastard who paints women head to toe in gold to feel like he’s bonking his own bullion.

Which brings me to the question tugging at every Bond fan’s cufflink. Now that Robert Pattinson has been confirmed as the new 007, he’ll need a gold-plated villain to go about “expecting” him.

So which actors would you like to see as the next Bond villain? We have some ideas.

Not famous enough, you say? Not got the chops for such a big-ticket role? Hasn’t earned it, has he? Pah, I’m here to say the actor-slash-musician has already played the best villain on television this year in MobLand’s Frankie Cooper. I’ve gushed about that performance on this website before, and I will again. He plays Cooper with the creeping, glassy-eyed energy of Jimmy Savile—all tracksuits and mirthful threats. But into that he stirs a dollop of Ben Kingsley’s Don “Yes you fucking will” Logan in Sexy Beast. There’s a light nod to Heath Ledger’s Joker there too. In all, it’s a monumental performance—laconically camp, cockney, impishly insane, and properly cackle-out-loud funny. For copyright reasons, I suppose we can’t expect a carbon copy of Frankie transplanted into Bond. But he offers the same vibe: a villain at once unsettling and amusing, which is the hardest trick in the Bond playbook.

There was something in the way he played Pennywise in the adaptation of Stephen King’s It that crawled under my skin like a hookworm. I think it’s the eyes. Bill Skarsgård has a mild lazy eye, which he uses in certain roles to create a disorienting swivel-eyed gaze. He used it for Pennywise. He’s a uniquely physical actor, too, well versed in roles that make children cry. He uses his body like a prop, twisting, deforming, looming, leering as the part demands. He played Count Orlok in Nosferatu, Eric Draven in The Crow and Marquis Vincent Bisset de Gramont in John Wick: Chapter 4. A small snag: he recently said he was “spent” playing bad guys. But he added that, if the right Bond villain landed on his desk, he’d be tempted out of baddie retirement for one last job. “[I] just want to go in and do one Mads Mikkelsen villain,” said the actor, referencing Mikkelsen’s character, Le Chiffre, in Casino Royale.

It’s time for a female lead villain. Sure, Bond has known plenty of miscreant women and seductresses, from Xenia Onatopp (GoldenEye) to Pussy Galore (Goldfinger). But few outright lead villains—hat tip to Lotte Lenya (Rosa Klebb in From Russia with Love) and Sophie Marceau (Elektra King in The World Is Not Enough). It’s a part for a proper actor, a doyenne of screen and stage. A dame, no less. Judi Dench played M across seven films as the one person Bond couldn’t charm, scold, or seduce his way past. Imagine that same withering, headmistress-like authority pointed the other way. Helen Mirren has the gravitas and the twinkle. She’s an Oscar winner who has played a queen, a gangster’s mum in the Fast & Furious films and, of course, the foul-mouthed matriarch Maeve Harrigan in MobLand. She would hardly break a sweat as a Bond villain.

Look at that face: the heavy brow, the gaunt cheeks, the dark, brooding eyes. It’s a face that looks like it was carved by candlelight—a mug made for lurking in the shadows. He’s got a bunch of baddies in his locker, from M. Bison in Street Fighter (2026) to Gareth, the Gemini Killer, in Dexter: Resurrection (2025). But perhaps his biggest ace in the hole is his turn as Piter De Vries in Denis Villeneuve’s Dune (2021). Villeneuve likes to work with actors he knows.

Oh, and the other thing: he really wants it. Dastmalchian explained why in a 2023 interview with /Film: “Trust me when I say that I can bring something to an enemy of 007, whether he or she or they be played in a way that no one has ever seen Bond brought to life before, which is, of course, the way that you guys always do it. I think there’s no one to push Bond to the limits of their capabilities the way that I could, and I would love to do it. So there’s my pitch to them. I can’t wait for them to read this.”

She’s already won an Oscar for playing a corporate villain in Michael Clayton, and she’s never met a prosthetic nose, fur collar or killer blow-dry she couldn’t weaponize... see Snowpiercer’s Minister Mason, Okja’s Lucy Mirando or the White Witch of Narnia—tick, tick, tick. She’s a chameleon with a stare that could freeze a martini.

More than that, Swinton is a master (mistress?) of the in-between, able to embrace and blur lines of gender, age, and species at the click of a slender finger. She possesses that rarest of powers: to change her physical presence, voice, and demeanor so completely as to render herself unrecognizable. Give her a lair, great hair and a long cigarette holder (maybe a Strawberry Ice vape—it’s 2026), and you have a thing of beauty and terror. Because if every great Bond villain needs a thing, Swinton’s is that she can be anything.

#6853. 35 Clever Tech Gifts That Will Upgrade Every Aspect of Their Day

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

From smart bird feeders to mug warmers, there’s something for everyone.

As much as we love a new throw pillow or vase under the Christmas tree, we know that some of the most coveted presents are electronic. Cool tech gifts can genuinely change your giftee’s life for the better. They can help them travel better, upgrade their home, get them through the workday faster, or just make them smile. So whoever you’re shopping for, from the chronically online teen to the discerning coworker, will appreciate these genius gifts.

Just don’t forget to personalize the item to your recipient. If they have interests such as photography and videography, consider getting them a tripod or GoPro camera so they can document all their action-packed adventures. The nature lover in your life will appreciate a smart bird feeder, and the chef will use the smart stand mixer all the time.

Below, we rounded up dozens of the coolest tech gifts on the market. Of course, we included tested and approved favorites from our very own staff. The technology that changed our lives will make for some super thoughtful gifts.

The Brick duo is kind of the anti-tech tech gift. If your loved one has been wanting to cut down their screen time, this is the smartest tool to help them do that. It works as a separation between you and your most distracting apps. One that can’t be dismissed without physical interaction. “I keep a Brick at my desk and in my kitchen as barriers to my doomscrolling habit,” Associate Shopping Editor Janae McKenzie says. “But the Brick Night is a real game-changer—it’s Bluetooth location-based, so the moment I cross the threshold into my bedroom, all my social apps are locked, so I actually have to go to sleep at a decent hour.”

Poplights are playful, renter-friendly wall sconces that can fit in any space; no electrician required. Instead of hardwiring, they use a rechargeable battery pack, so you can display the warm light and vibrant color with no ugly wires in sight. Installation is as easy as attaching a few Command strips.

The steamer is the new wrinkle-releasing mainstay, leaving the iron in the past. But instead of merely spraying steam, the Nori Cloud Max comes with vacuum technology that pulls the clothes toward the steamer. This smooths out your clothes with way fewer passes.

This is the ultimate travel tech accessory for frequent fliers. “Any overpacker knows how valuable suitcase space is,” McKenzie says. “I bought two of these travel bags because of the genius mini-vacuum that comes with them. They work like space-saving bags, but small enough to fit in a carry-on and take on the go. No more calls to the hotel front desk for a vacuum.”

The easiest way to make your bathroom feel like a spa? Add a towel warmer. Stepping out of the shower, you can retrieve a fluffy towel from this heater to wrap up in, easing the harsh cold of tile floors. It comes with a timer, so you won’t have to worry about leaving it on overnight.

Yes, landlines are cool again. This retro receiver attachment plugs into your phone’s USB-C port, so when you pick up a call, you can have a more tactile experience. It comes with a mic and speaker, so it’s primed for everything from your next Zoom meeting to long-distance catch-up chats.

This bouncy LED lamp comes in three colors and two sizes. The spring-loaded body adds some whimsy to your home office. It lights up with a soft touch and provides up to eight hours of warm glow.

Canopy is known for its powerful water filtration, air purification, and humidifiers, but its latest launch is the most versatile. The inline shower filter is designed to work with any showerhead, from handheld extensions to rain showers. It’s compact and removes contaminants for cleaner skin and air.

Not everyone has the square footage for a full-scale smart fridge, so Rocco just launched a mini solution to that problem. The Mini Mini is sized for your desktop, countertop, or nightstand. Perfect for bedtime skin care essentials or a midday Diet Coke break.

We forget how stale the air quality gets. Birdie can help you remember. The mechanical indicator droops when you need to crack a window and let in some fresh air. It doesn’t hurt that the simple Danish design is so chic.

This speaker is a built-in party starter. It’s waterproof and drop-proof, so it’s perfect to bring to the beach, pool, or anywhere you need some powerful tunes. The steady bass and 28 hours of playtime keep up with any gathering. It’s even a built-in power bank.

No one knows they need an electric candle lighter until they have one. Take it camping, or just have a safer way to enjoy your candle collection. The slim, rose-gold profile is cute enough to leave on display.

We're not the first to call a desk pad "sexy," but something about this sleek Grove Made piece gives off that exact vibe. It's made from natural materials like pine rosin and wood flour, making it durable, antistatic, and fingerprint-resistant. The pad comes in a variety of eye-pleasing colors and five different sizes to elevate any office space.

You don't have to be an avid bird watcher to enjoy this smart feeder. Armed with a smart camera, the feeder can take photos of birds as they come to feast on the seeds, alert you with bird arrival notifications, and identify up to 1,000 species so you can learn about the types that drop by for a snack. You can attach Bird Buddy to a tree, pole, wall, or fence.

"Fellow's sleek Stagg EKG electric kettle is now undoubtedly the coolest thing in my kitchen," says tester Katelyn Lunders. "I used to put my stovetop kettle on while I was getting ready in the morning and then sprint to turn it off when it started screaming at me, but Fellow's kettle is free of an annoying whistle (and requires no cardio). Instead, it has a touch screen that allows you to pick your perfect temperature and then quickly brings the water to that point and holds it there until you're ready to pour it over your coffee or tea. A true revelation for hot beverage lovers everywhere."

"When I first met my husband's parents, I gifted his dad a digital picture frame so that he can keep up with our lives in NYC and travels (they live in Virginia)," says Senior Commerce Editor Marina Liao. "These types of frames are so convenient because you can upload photos directly from your phone, from anywhere in the world, and they will populate into the digital frame." The best part about Skylight is that should the Wi-Fi go down, this frame will still display the photos (you just can't receive anything until the connection is restored).

“I wanted a private proposal with just my now-husband and our dog, so he bought this tripod so we would be able to capture the moment,” says Liao. “It comes with a tiny remote, is easy to set up, and packs up well into a small bag. Though we don’t use the selfie ring light much, it is very bright and illuminating, so it’s perfect for content creators or if you take photos in poorly lit spots.”

A GoPro is the perfect gift for the adventure seeker in your life. Some features to note about this waterproof model: the hindsight captures 30 seconds before you hit record so you don't miss a moment, and it has a new and improved slow-mo capability, plus TimeWrap 3.0 and night lapse.

For the person who's always trying to repair or build electronics from scratch, they will love this all-encompassing tech toolkit. It includes a 64 precision bit driver set, tweezers, flex extension, opening tools, and an antistatic wristband housed in a polycarbonate case.

#6852. Are You a Super Ager? This Competition Will Be the Judge of That

Alan Krepack stands with his hands on his hips and takes a deep breath as he sizes up the Over Under: an obstacle with a high wooden box and low wooden beam spread 10 meters apart. Music booms over the speakers and other competitors shuffle around the cavernous room of the Armory Track in Washington Heights, New York, but Krepack stays laser-focused, thanks to years of meditation practice.

As soon as the volunteer says “go” he sprints to the box, plants his hands, and hoists his feet up and over. As he lands on the other side, he sprints to the beam, dropping down into an army crawl. “Take your time, take your time,” his son yells from the sidelines. Krepack reaches the far end, rushes back, and repeats the sequence again before crossing the line in 51 seconds. To cool down, he paces back and forth with his hands on his head. “I think I’m a sprinter, I’m a jumper, but then it’s like, fuck, no, I’m 73 years old,” he says, catching his breath.

The challenge is just the first of eight trials Krepeck will tackle throughout the day alongside his sons, Harrison and Aaron, at the Super Age Games, a fitness and mental competition designed to assess abilities associated with healthy aging. On November 7th, the inaugural day-long event drew 530 athletes from 42 states and 15 countries, and about 70% of participants were between the ages of 40 and 64. Krepeck agreed to let me shadow him to understand this new event through the lens of someone who is, in fact, aging, and would like to do so gracefully.

The name of the Games comes from a buzzword that originated in the longevity world: super ager. The term was originally coined by Northwestern University researchers to describe adults over 80 whose memories rival those of people 30 years younger. These days it’s thrown around on podcasts and in books to describe anyone who defies both the typical trajectory of both cognitive and physical aging.

The inaugural Super Age Games drew 530 athletes from 42 states and 15 countries.

From crawling across a cargo net to reading microexpressions, the challenges in the competition measure longevity markers like functional strength, aerobic capacity, balance, and cognitive skill. At the end, athletes see where they rank among their age group and receive their “Super Age,” learning how their body’s physical fitness stacks up against the general U.S. population’s.

In an era where your average middle-aged midwestern dad is training for Hyrox, and a new, outlandishly extreme challenge is being invented every few months, the Super Age games are a low-key counterpoint to raw, stunty, athleticism, born from the metric-obsessed fitness subcultures who prize longevity above all. And now that fitness trackers like Whoop or Apple Watch can track VO2max, heart rate variability, and resting heart rates, that group continues to grow. Along the way, these stats have become status symbols on par with owning a Lamborghini. And now that overall health is quantifiable, fitness values have become less about just how fast you run or how much you lift, but how slowly aging.

It follows that, despite its name, Super Age Games isn’t just for aging athletes. The crowd there leaned middle-aged, and the youngest person at the competition was 21. As we stood by the dead hang station, Krepak pointed across the room at a brolic young man who looked like he could be an NFL prospect: “That dude’s a serious athlete,” he says, as we watched him run around the track cradling a sandbag on his shoulder.

Unlike competitions like Hyrox, where athletes race through a sequence of workouts against the clock, the Super Age Games allows competitors to rest as long as they need between trials. In fact, Junior Kennedy, the fitness program director for the Games, encourages it. “There's no rush,” he says. “The goal is to get through each event as intentionally as you can. We need to understand what it means to recover.”

Each trial was developed with a certain longevity marker in mind, and developed with input from researchers at the Buck Institute for Research on Aging, a leading nonprofit research center based in Novato, California dedicated to understanding aging. The Over Under, for example, tests agility by judging how fast you can change levels and switch directions. Meanwhile, the Synapse, a challenge where participants track moving balls on a screen and identify them after they've been shuffled around, is meant to measure cognitive processing.

Challenges in the competition measure longevity markers like functional strength, aerobic capacity, balance, and cognitive skill.

Krepack, a psychologist and lifelong athlete from Mill Valley, California, first heard about the event through its founder, David Stewart. Stewart is a media founder behind two niche longevity platforms, AGEIST and Super Age, where he writes about longevity, wellness, and redefining aging (one of his website testimonial’s says he has made aging “cool and aspirational.”). Krepack’s generally a skeptic of the profit-driven modern wellness industry—“I've always had a jaded view of all of that stuff,” he says—but he has followed both Stewart and the Buck Institutes’ work for years.

The competition appealed to Krepack, in part because he’s spent decades cultivating a consistent exercise routine he’s proud of. He played baseball in college and has spent much of his life skiing, cycling, and playing other sports. These days, his schedule revolves around a handful of rituals: bike rides on Mondays and Fridays, a Saturday hike with a friend, gardening, yoga, and Zen meditation most days. When he signed up for the Games in June, he altered his exercise routine, lifted more weights, practiced hanging from a bar, and experimented with the Neurotracker cognitive test online.

Yet all that training didn’t prepare Krepack for the biggest surprise of his day. After scoring well on both the Inferno (the assault bike VO2max test) and Synapse, next came the fourth trial: the “Tightrope,” a balance challenge that requires competitors to walk along a low beam while carrying a water-filled bag, taking as many consecutive steps as they can in two minutes without falling. He had expected to excel, thanks to years of skiing and yoga. Instead, he struggled to keep steady as the water shifted inside the bag. As his foot slipped off the bar, his eyes got wide. “I can’t believe my balance,” he says. “That’s my one disappointment in life now.”

Alan Krepack's sons Harrison and Aaron during the “tightrope” challenge.

Krepack was still hard on himself as we walked over to the dead hang trial. But after beating both his sons’ times, he seemed to get his competitive spirit back. He pushed through the remaining challenge determined to finish strong. And he did: By the end of the Games, he had placed second overall, with scores adjusted for age.

A few days after the competition, I checked in with Krepack to ask him how he’d processed the event. He responded with screenshots of his scores. His highest score came from the cargo net, which he credited to sheer determination rather than any particular finesse: “due to my will and overcoming an unconventional and desperate style!” he texted me, “Plus my sons were great support and focus partners.”

But the Games were also a reality check about aging: “I left there feeling both young and old,” he tells me on a phone call. As he watched his sons rocket through the obstacles, he was forced to confront how much of his athleticism he’d lost. “They just flew over it. They were so fluid, and so fast,” he says. “I’m not 30 or 40 or 50 or 60 anymore.”

Despite all the longevity industry's promises of maintaining youth, Krepack sees little value in denying the inevitable. Aging well, he says, is about “accepting, improving, and doing your best. It’s not a denial of who you are at this point in life.” The goal, he says, is to make the most of what you’re still capable of.
