#6850. “It Was Never About Fashion”: Emily in Paris’s Costume Designer Reveals the Method to Her Madness

“Emily in Paris: Fashion Without Rules,” a new show at SCAD Fashion in Atlanta, is devoted to the series’s unique style

Emily in Paris premiered when audiences needed it most. The Netflix original, created by Sex and the City producer Darren Star, offered an escapist fantasy: a young girl trying to find love and success in Paris in October 2020. The show functioned as a reprieve from the pandemic, a dreamy alternative in which we could vicariously live through Emily (Lily Collins) as she gallivanted around the City of Light. It was and still is delectably digestible television.

The love for Emily in Paris was anything but unanimous. Critics wasted no time tearing the show apart, with some targeting its depictions of French culture or claiming the script lacked the bite of Star’s previous television shows. The criticism extended to Emily’s idiosyncratic wardrobe, with some writing that the clothing leaned hard on American stereotypes of French fashion or looked cartoonish, with clashing patterns and bright colors. Costume designer Marylin Fitoussi took these criticisms head-on and doubled down. Emily’s outfits weren’t meant to be couture or designer; they were meant to dress a girl finding her sense of self, expressing her desire to explore the unknown.

For six seasons, Fitoussi curated a wardrobe fit for Emily, a bright-eyed young woman unafraid to try something new, to break the rules. This mirrors Fitoussi’s personal philosophy on clothing: that fashion is meant to break rules, to push boundaries, to provide a space of unrelenting experimentation. Ahead of the sixth season, SCAD FASH Museum of Fashion + Film compiles six seasons’ worth of outfits in “Emily in Paris: Fashion Without Rules,” on view through March 14, 2027. The exhibition lives up to its name; for the designer, clothing is a mode of expression that lets anyone feel free, wherever they are. It offers a character study, tracing Emily’s evolution from a doe-eyed expat to a well-versed Parisian resident. (And worry not, there are also looks from Emily’s chic boss Sylvie—played with devastating elegance by Philippine Leroy-Beaulieu.)

Ahead of season 6, Bazaar sat down with Fitoussi at SCAD Fashion in Atlanta to discuss working with Lily Collins, her swan song for Emily in Paris, and how everyone has the potential for creativity.

Marylin Fitoussi: I don’t recall a contemporary TV show being in the spotlight in this way. Generally, when you think of an exhibition, you think of period pieces or couture pieces. The show is really part of pop culture now!

MF: I was surprised when fashion magazines [criticized the show] at the beginning. I’m not a fashion designer, [but] I love fashion and I love clothes. This show was not about fashion at the beginning. [Emily] is a bubbly character with a very strong personality from Chicago; she couldn’t have come dressed in Comme des Garçons or in Yamamoto. I needed to have this cultural shock. She’s a fish-out-of-water character, and we needed something to catch the audience’s eyes. When you want to show people that you love the culture but you can’t express it because you don’t know the codes, you don’t speak the language, the only way you find it is through clothes. It was never about “fashion.” Sometimes, it’s about creativity, individuality, making something not trendy at all. The audience got that, and fashion magazines didn’t get it, so they killed me.

MF: Many times, I’ve asked myself, “Why does this little gingham suit have so much influence?” It’s quite simple. It was the most duplicated, copied, reposted, fan-arted, and used as a Halloween costume. It touched people because it was for them. The French can have their own version of it, their own concept, but for the entire world, it was this one little girly, timeless outfit with the red beret, the red lips, the red soul.

MF: She read the bad press after the release of season 1, and we had a talk. I said they hated it in France; they find it hideous. [I asked her] “Do you want me to change [it]?” She was smiling and said, “No, we can’t, and we won’t please everybody. We need to keep this strong DNA. We need to continue to have this unexpected silhouette because it’s what catches the audience’s eye.” It’s a guilty pleasure. We can say, I love it.

It opens a way for the audience to see fashion in a different way, where you can mix in an awkward way and free people from this law where you shouldn’t [do that]. Coco Chanel used to say, “Before leaving a room, look at yourself in the mirror and take something out.” I always say, “Put something more on.”

MF: I wanted to present a more elevated version, more mature, more evolved, but still true to what she was and what she is, with an eclectic combination and a little bit awkward. She knows the French rules, but as I do, she finds French fashion so boring. In general, I find that fashion is less and less interesting. Why are fashion and celebrity stylists sourcing more and more from archives? What it’s showing is that now everything is the same. Everything is boring, and I understand why. They need to sell the show! We don’t have to sell anything. We have to sell dreams, illusions, craziness, boldness. People watch the show in groups. Some people say, “I love it,” and some say, “I hate it,” and they have a dialogue and fight and have a good [time]. We love that.

MF: A young designer wants to be famous right away. Fame doesn’t happen, and it doesn’t matter. You need to be happy with what you’re doing. With costume design versus fashion design, you can be much freer. You don’t have anything to sell, and you don’t have to design the “It bag.” If your show is successful, it’s a win-win for everybody. To see that, you need to be true to your vision. You need to be true to your gut feeling, to your vision, to your DNA. Talent is not enough; you need to be at the right place at the right time. Being discovered by Darren Star … he changed my life. At some point, you find the right project for you.

You need to work a lot to take care of every detail. You need to be strong and push the boundaries. You need to try to show a different way to see costumes and clothes, to see that everything is possible…. Style begins when fear ends. You don’t want to please everybody, and I’m always attracted to people who dress in a different way, and I’m never judgmental. To create something is to expose yourself. It’s very vulnerable. It’s brave.

This interview has been condensed and edited for clarity.

#6848. How to Care for a Thanksgiving Cactus so It Blooms Right on Time

Here's what experts say you need to know about this low-maintenance plant.

Want an easy-care plant to brighten your home through fall and winter? Skip the usual mums and try a Thanksgiving cactus. Though it’s native to Brazil, this plant looks nothing like a desert cactus: Its flat, toothed stems end in pointed tips, and its vivid tubular flowers usually appear in late November. That timing is how it got its name. It’s related to Easter and Christmas cacti, which flower around their namesake holidays.

Thanksgiving cacti can grow outdoors in USDA Hardiness Zones 10–12, but they’re well suited to indoor life in any climate. Their bright flowers come in colors including red, pink, white, and yellow, adding a cheerful burst of color to a room.

Wondering how to help yours thrive? Here’s what to know about growing and caring for a Thanksgiving cactus.

The Thanksgiving cactus loves bright spots that aren't in direct sunlight. Too much sun can cause the plant to turn yellow rather than its beautiful green, so keeping it by a north- or east-facing window or even a shady south- or west-facing window is ideal. For your plant to produce the best flowers, it shouldn't receive more than 12 hours of light. It needs the darkness to produce blooms.

All holiday cacti require a bit more magnesium than other houseplants, so finding your Thanksgiving cactus a magnesium-rich fertilizer you can add to a well-draining soil is essential. The Home and Garden Information Center (HGIC) suggests supplementing its water with Epsom salts—try mixing in one teaspoon of Epsom salts per gallon of water. But avoid doing so during the week you add the fertilizer, which you should do once a month. Stop adding fertilizer in the late summer months for better flower production in the fall.

Thanksgiving cacti should be planted in a pot with drainage holes at the bottom since they won't thrive in soggy soil. Consider adding peat moss or sand to the pot to prevent pooling water around the roots. Thanksgiving cacti are root-bound, so there's no need to repot them for about two to three years.

The soil surrounding your Thanksgiving cactus should never be soaking wet or bone dry. However, the soil should be consistently moist and never fully dry to the touch. Holiday cacti are prone to root rot, so standing water is your main enemy. During the hot months, they can stand to be slightly underwatered.

Like most tropical plants, Thanksgiving cacti thrive in a humid, warm environment. However, the typical household temperature can keep a cactus very healthy too. The temperature shouldn't get above 80 degrees Fahrenheit or below 55 degrees, though ideally you should keep your cactus between 75 and 80 degrees during the day, according to the University of Wisconsin-Madison's Horticulture Department.

Pruning is an important task for plant lovers to keep any houseplant healthy, and Thanksgiving cacti are no different. To promote bud growth, cut the stems in the late spring or early summer, and make sure you have the right gardening tools for the job. Be sure to save the cut stems for propagation too!

If you want more of these tropical cacti around your house, maybe for a huge, flowering centerpiece for the holidays, use cut stems to produce more plants. HGIC suggests using stems with three to five phylloclades (or stem joints) on each to start. Set the segments aside for a day to dry out, then stick them about one inch deep into a pot with moist soil. Do this only in the spring and summer while the plant is actively growing, and avoid doing it in the winter once it has bloomed.

Because the Thanksgiving cactus is native to Brazil, you should try to mimic its natural environment as best you can to get it to bloom, which is near the end of the rainy season. The gardening department at Michigan State University Extension explains that for the buds to develop on your indoor plant, Thanksgiving cacti need at least 13 hours of total darkness each day, bright light when they're not covered up or hidden away, and a consistent temperature ranging between 55 and 65 degrees. You should start this process in late September or early October if you want blooms around the holiday season.

Once the plant has flowered for the season, then you can go back to taking care of it the way you normally would with the help of the guidelines written above.

According to PetMD, Thanksgiving cacti are not toxic to dogs and cats, unlike another holiday plant, the poinsettia. However, that doesn't mean you should let your furry friends munch on it. If large amounts are consumed, your pets can experience vomiting, diarrhea, and a decreased appetite.

#6847. Why Don’t You … Take Fashion Inspiration from the World’s Great Performers?

Diana Vreeland had a singular take on not only fashion but life. Her eccentricity has become folklore and was best exemplified in an illustrated column of irreverent advice for chic living she called “Why Don’t You?” Resurrected and reimagined for modern life, the column, now written by Bazaar’s executive digital director, Lynette Nylander, will dispense tips on how to live stylishly, decadently, and daringly.

Here’s a thought: Don’t get dressed. Mount a production. Put on a show. Go on tour. And if the reviews are lukewarm, don’t change the costume; blame the critic! For the performance issue, Lynette Nylander takes cues from the stage’s most stylish to arm you with tips for stepping out.

that the “effortless” look took 40 minutes. The “I just threw it on” leather jacket, the smudged eye makeup, the shirt that looks slept in: All of it is stagecraft. Miley Cyrus’s new rock-star look personifies this. She’s not someone to shy away from an “era,” and this one is all tousled hair, loads of lashes, and leather and lace from the likes of Tom Ford, Roberto Cavalli, and Saint Laurent. Effortless? No. Fabulous? Absolutely!

some noise. Taffeta rustles, silk whispers, embellishments jangle, and leather creaks with intent. Performance is not only visual. The swish of a skirt on a staircase is part of the entrance, and the weight of crepe hugging the hips is part of the walk. The late, great Tina Turner was a master of clothes that created a symphony as she writhed around the stage; often, they were the creations of the late, great Bob Mackie. the precision of a uniform. Even people who wear the same thing every day are making a statement, and the strongest of them curate that repetition with real rigor. Consistency, done well, is a kind of theater. Just don’t make it boring! Consider Steve Jobs, who famously wore only Issey Miyake turtlenecks and Levi’s. Truly the only tech titan to ever have taste!

the art of quick change. For Céline Dion’s fall residency at the Plénitude Arena in Paris, underneath the spotlight wasn’t the only site where a performance took place. Like all true divas, Dion knows that every moment of every day is an opportunity to perform. Outside of the Royal Monceau hotel, her home in Paris, where her faithful fans lined up to greet her, she treated onlookers to a dance, a song, an air-kiss, and always a fashion show: off-the-runway looks from Jacquemus, Loewe, and Phoebe Philo. As they say, all the world’s a stage.

the ordinary garment its moment. Not every act needs sequins. A perfect white tee, a pair of jeans that have taken on the shape of a life, a forever leather jacket worn with confidence: these are the understudies who steal the show. Performance in fashion is not always about drama. Sometimes it is the quiet authority of a simple thing worn exactly right. A disciple of this way of dressing? Kim Gordon, whose effortless style, both onstage and offstage, has made her your favorite celebrity’s favorite celebrity for the past 40 years.

#6845. Pride and Prejudice’s Rufus Sewell Is the Most Dashing Man on Television

It’s possible that Rufus Sewell is having too much fun right now. Between playing morally ambiguous Vice President (and husband to the U.S. Ambassador to the Court of St. James’s) Hal Wyler in Netflix’s frothy, prestige-y series The Diplomat and the beloved but deeply flawed Mr. Bennet in the streamer’s upcoming adaptation of Pride and Prejudice, he’s cornered the market on lovable rascals. He thrives on “exercises in gray” and relishes “when it goes wrong” for a character. “It’s the opposite of what you want in life,” he tells me. “You want drama, you want mistakes.” These roles offer plenty of both.

Wyler, of The Diplomat, is navigating how to work with the increasingly autocratic and bloody-minded President Grace Penn (Allison Janney) thousands of miles away from his wife Kate (Keri Russell), who is starting to exhibit some of his own manipulative behaviors she claims to abhor. Two centuries prior, Mr. Bennet is reckoning not with geopolitical crises and egomaniacal heads of state, but with the challenges his wife faces in marrying off five eligible, if dowry-poor, daughters.

Sewell sat down for a long chat with GQ about the two series, the value of blending high silliness with high stakes in the same scene, and how bodily functions are an under-appreciated key to unlocking a character’s specificity.

Rufus Sewell: The interesting question for me, for the second half of the season, was how much is Hal truly aware of everything that Kate and everyone else are noticing and concerned about with Grace's behavior. How much does he see? How much is he choosing to pretend not to see in order to stay in the room? He's always believed that access is all, but how much do you compromise in order to stay in the room? Where’s the point when you realize you stayed in the room too long without saying anything, and the moment’s passed, and you are just an accomplice? I found it very difficult to play his seeming unctuousness and obsequiousness with Grace this season, and to not be trying to protect my character in the wrong way, because I felt he was failing.

He’s overestimated his ability to effect change with Grace. Hal was prepared to appear to be supporting her, when he believed in his ability to steer her in a better direction, and I think he goes past that point. When I read the final episode script and saw where he ends up walking out, it gave me the courage to really lean into how much he goes wrong, by his own standards earlier in the season. He begins to lose his way in a way that he never has done before. But I embraced it, knowing that the real gift of the part was getting to play him going wrong and then seemingly correcting himself. We actually shot a speech that he made, just to give us a sense of where the scene was headed and for me to know how to play it. They might end up using some of it in the next season, or they could end up completely changing what was written. Hal coming out of the summit is a momentous decision in terms of what that does for the future and I've got no idea what’s next—I can't wait to start filming season five to find out.

I was always shocked when this show was described as a thriller because I always thought it was a kind of romantic sex comedy with extremely high stakes, because what it reminded me of firstly was some of the comedy of divorce screwball films of the 1940s. The characters may be divorced after a very tempestuous relationship, but they’re interlinked in a way that can never be undone. There's a romance to all of that history.

The other thing that it reminded me of was the kind of films that were made in the '70s—French films where halfway through an argument one of the characters would sit down on the toilet, you know what I mean? The people inside these films remind you of your own relationships. Your long-term relationship might not go to the same extremes, it might not be in the same setting, but I think people are people. What I love about their relationship is their shared wonkiness, and the getting into the nitty-gritty of the politics and taking someone down and hatching a plot whilst fighting over pastries and getting crumbs on the bed—all of that is part of their foreplay.

That is what the show is about, as far as I'm concerned. These are people who wear suits that might stand at a lectern and be on TV, but the fact is, how do you scratch an itch? Do you smell? Do you need to go to the toilet? These are the things that the script leans into a lot, and it might put some people off, but I don't care about that. That's the thing that makes it feel real, and it's so refreshing to play.

He'll flirt with anyone. We all know people like this. It's not even necessarily sexual, he just engages with people on that level and it's a natural thing for him.

I would say Hal’s flirtatiousness being a problem is open for debate, because a lot of what we understand of him is via Kate’s descriptions of him and his behavior. I can and often do see it in a different way. Part of the story of seasons three and four is Kate’s realization that a lot of stuff she thought of as his personality was in fact the mantle of the job that he'd taken on, because we see her doing the same kinds of things now that she is an ambassador. They both have a lot of the same qualities. They're both killers in a sense, and it's been very convenient for her and great for the show for her to introduce the public to Hal through her descriptions. And yet, every time it seems like he's changed character, in fact, it is revealing something from a different perspective.

We learned pretty early on that we were going to be doing some very, very long scenes, and we were given the advantage of seeing the scenes very early so we could slowly gestate the performances. We needed that because Windsor Castle in the show was comprised of several different buildings and locations, some of them with oceans between them, so we’d be shooting some parts the next day, and other bits in four months’ time.

Especially with a two-hander, with such long takes, you can go in and out of being relaxed, unrelaxed, free, slightly bound, and actually you get to the point where you stop worrying about it. It's like being on stage, because you end up too tired to be bad all the time and end up inhabiting the moment and things just happen. I love that all of it was a surprise to us.

I think what is most important in this particular iteration of the relationship between Mr. and Mrs. Bennet is that he comes to realize which ways he's failed his daughters. When Mrs. Bennet says to him in this version, it's easy for you to say that I’m silly or frivolous, but you try being a woman at this age with no means—this is the fight of their lives that he is making these jokes about. And yes, of course he's funny and right in certain ways, but he's also guilty of negligence of his family for the sake of an easy life for himself. The reason they have no money is that so much money has been spent on bonnets. Money could have been saved, but in order to have a quiet life, he's ducked out of fights in order to avoid responsibility.

Exactly. It was a gift in this role to have that moment when he feels that, admits that, and gets to be slightly humbled in this idea of himself that he's been cultivating. He didn’t actively or consciously avoid having this revelation, but that’s the effect of staying in his study, and now he’s facing the consequences. To use very modern terms, he has a revelation of what his pattern has been. It made sense!

One could be forgiven for thinking that Mr. and Mrs. Bennet are two people that used to be attracted to each other and now have nothing in common and don't like each other. I think it's born out in the book, though, the idea that there is still a love there, and especially a shared humor between them, and a bit of a spark remains.

I love the fact that we get to get drunk together and stuff like that. Olivia Colman and I worked on The Father together a few years ago with Anthony Hopkins, which was a very different kind of gig, but as is often the case with things that are very heavy and intense, we had a hoot doing it. I loved working with her, and was very excited about the chance to work with her again. I love the scenes between Mr. Bennet and Mrs. Bennet. They were such a treat for us.

#6843. 20 White Elephant Gifts Everyone Will Fight Over

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Perfect for that unnecessary work exchange.

If you’re reading this, there’s probably a holiday gift exchange on your calendar coming up. Maybe your boss organized one for the office, or maybe it’s the doing of that one friend who loves hosting so much they’d throw a party for Arbor Day. Either way, you’re probably wondering: What the heck do I bring to this thing?

There are a few rules to this game. First, there’s likely a price cap, so we’re keeping everything under $100. (If your white elephant involves luxury gifts, we’d love to be a fly on that very well-appointed wall.) Second, your gift should be something just about anyone would be excited to unwrap. That rules out clothes and shoes with specific sizes, phone cases for whatever iPhone model is currently in circulation, and franchise merch that assumes your giftee has seen every Marvel movie or follows football.

#6841. Pink Hair Is Back and Everyone From SZA to Adéla Are Making a Case for It

Remember when Kylie Jenner was changing her hair color about every other week, Tumblr was overflowing with pastel-haired girlies, and we were all contemplating whether we could pull off pink hair? Well, dust off your 2016 Pinterest boards, bb, because pink hair is officially making a comeback. But this time, it’s not just King Kylie leading the charge. SZA, Adéla, and even Martha Stewart have been putting their own spins on the color, proving that there’s no one way (or age!) to enter your pink hair era. “Since pink is not a natural hair color, you don’t really have to abide by any typical color analysis rules,” says colorist Emily Claire, which means you have free range to do whatever the heck you’d like.

And the range is range-ing. From electric pink to soft rose gold, I promise there’s a rosy hue for you. Personally, I’m loving this return to hair color that’s just fun—and I’m not the only one. “Recently my clients with pink hair have been requesting baby pink and deep berry pink,” says colorist Cara Craig. Plus, Cosmo called out pink as a top fall hair color trend this year. So, whether you’re ready to go full-on Barbie or want to experiment with a subtle rosy tint, there’s a pink hair idea with your name on it. Ahead, we’re breaking it all down, including how to choose your shade, what to ask for at the salon, and how to keep your pink hair looking fresh.

Short answer: Yes. There’s a truly pink hair color for everyone. “I have clients asking for all shades of pink, from blush and rose gold to hot pink to magenta,” says hairstylist Alyssa Chapa.

Pink is surprisingly so versatile, so there are plenty of ways to make it work for you. “In my opinion, pink is a neutral. I think of fashion colors as cousins to classic shades,” says Craig. “So if you are a blonde, consider baby pink. If you are a brunette you’ll want to move to darker pinks and fuchsias. If you want to feel like a redhead then wear rose golds and warm pinks.” She also recommends a violet pink if you prefer keeping things cool-toned.

Wondering about how your skin tone will look with pink hair? “I’m personally of the opinion that there is no right or wrong shade for your skin tone,” says Chapa, but if it’s really important to you, here are her tips for finding the best hair color for your skin tone. “Start by figuring out your undertones,” she says. “I like using the veins on the inside of the wrist; if you have blue or purple veins then you usually have cool undertones, green veins mean warm undertones, and a mixture of both is a neutral undertone.” Then, it’s pretty easy from there: Match your undertones to the shade of pink, which your colorist will be able to help out with (think baby or icy pink for cool tones, peach or salmon pink for warm tones, that kind of thing).

First things first: Bring reference photos to your colorist—we’ve handily included some pics throughout this story for ya. After all, Adéla’s vivid pink and Stewart’s subtle rose gold hair require very different approaches, so you’ll want to make sure you’re specific about what shade you’re after.

“For most people, unless you happen to be naturally blonde, the process would involve lightening your hair to the desired level with bleach, then toning it to the desired pink with a direct dye,” says Claire.

Need some specific pink hair ideas? These are colorist-approved: “One of my favorite ways to wear pink lately is over classic blonde highlights,” says Craig. “I also love a pink double process (e.g. Adéla).” Double process just means a two-step coloring method—your colorist will first bleach/lighten your hair, then apply the pink color. You can also go with chunky highlights, “especially a halo hairline where the pink frames the face,” adds Chapa. “Mixing chunky pink highlights in with shades of red or brown to create a kind of calico effect is also popular online.”

Now for the not-so-fun part: Pink hair doesn’t necessarily equal low-maintenance hair. “No matter the tone, you’re going to have to refresh it in four to six weeks if you want it to stay pink,” says Chapa. If you want to go as low-maintenance as possible, a softer pink is the way to go, notes Craig.

For a more natural-looking grow-out or to keep your hair color easy, “your alternatives are to choose an already muted color like a dusty rose or mauve and let it fade to blonde or brunette, or to invest in an at-home color depositing treatment to maintain your pink until your next salon visit,” Chapa says.

You can help extend your color by washing less frequently, using color-safe shampoo and conditioner, limiting heat styling, and incorporating a color-depositing mask or at-home hair gloss. “I always recommend my clients use a pink-tinted shampoo or conditioner to keep up with the color between appointments at home,” says Claire.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmo. She’s covered all things skincare, makeup, hair, and more, including trending haircuts and hair colors you have to try.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmopolitan and was previously the beauty assistant at Good Housekeeping, Woman's Day, and Prevention.

#6836. How to Grow a Beard

So you want to learn how to grow a beard. First off, you should know that it helps to have the right genetics. Second, you have to stay committed to the endeavor. Too often, beard-growing attempts end before they’ve even had a chance to establish itself.

“I’d say give it at least three to four weeks of solid growth before you even think about touching it with clippers,” Victor Mendoza, co-founder of beard care brand Viking Revolution, says. “Your beard needs time to actually show you what it’s going to look like, and that growth period lets you see the natural direction everything’s growing in. Plus, some hairs take longer to come in than others, so patience is key.”

There’s a sweet spot between letting your facial hair grow out and knowing when it’s time to take the trimmers for the best results possible, even if you can’t go full lumberjack. These are the best tips to maximize your hair follicles’ potential whether this is your first beard or your fiftieth.

There are many different beard styles to choose from; you don’t need to stick to neatly manicured, uniform look. Let your growth be influenced by the style you want to achieve.

“Your genes determine hair thickness, growth rate, density, and color,” Mendoza says. “If your dad or granddad had a thin beard, you’re probably dealing with the same hand, and that’s just life.”

If your beard is patchy, or if it has a unique growth pattern (sparse on the cheeks, full on the goatee, for example), then you should set your expectations properly. This goes for beards big, small, and medium alike. And while nutrition is important for hair growth, you can’t cheat genetics with your diet.

Patience is a virtue. Not only can a little time help fill in some patchiness, but it’s also requisite for almost any style. A beard transforms into something new every week. So, if it’s seeming like you’ll never reach your goal—in particular if it’s a long beard—then you honestly might wake up one morning and realize that you’ve arrived at the destination.

Mendoza says to wait at least two weeks before you start to do a cleanup, but only around the ends, i.e. the hairs that aren’t within the perimeter in which you want your beard to grow. That’ll help you figure out your beard’s natural shape.

One of the tenets of beard maintenance is to keep it defined. In short: shave the neckline and clean up the cheek lines. These keep it more beard-like and less neckbeard-like—unless, for some reason, a wooly stoner beard is your aim. In that case, let it grow wild and free.

The second way to keep things looking intentional is to sculpt and trim your beard as it grows. It might feel counterproductive—and counter to #2—but it’s the same way you need to get haircuts while growing out your hair. As Mendoza says, this helps direct the hair exactly where it needs to be: Fuller in some spots, less so in others. The styling products in the next tip can of course help influence things, but you foremost want to keep hairs in check as needed: This can mean spot-checking strays, snipping away split ends, or simply taking out some bulk. You’ll want a beard trimmer or a sharp pair of scissors.

There are a handful of beard products you should consider picking up if you’re serious about proper care and maintenance. A few drops of beard oil will absorb quickly and give your hair the right nutrients (plus a pinch of shine), while also making it less wild and frizzy. You can also swap in a beard balm or cream, something slightly heftier than an oil, to help style the beard and keep it in place all day. What’s important is that you clean your beard every day with an actual beard wash and not just a regular shampoo, which Mendoza says will dry out your beard and your face.

Weekly maintenance will consist of a deep condition, which you can do in the shower. “If you’re doing those things consistently, your beard is going to look way healthier and fuller than most guys out there,” he says.

Beard brushes help exfoliate the skin beneath the beard, for starters, which lifts dead skin cells and prevents future flaking. Moreover, brushing your beard at the end of each day will ensure its health and tame-ability in the long haul. This distributes the natural oils produced in the skin, which otherwise collect at the base of the shaft and only nourish the bottom of each hair. This oil distribution prevents breakage, frizzing, and split ends. (This is a great time to apply a few drops of beard oil as a booster, especially after a shower.)

Once your base has grown out—this can take months—it’s time to really make it yours. One of our favorites is to snip the bridge between the mustache and the chin, thus marooning the mustache. Or, pare back the beard itself, leaving a fuller mustache. You have lots of options, and it’s your privilege as a beard grower to flaunt it.

#6835. What’s in Her Pockets? Gemma Chan Kept Snacks in Her Pink Valentino Dress at the Oscars

The star of Josephine answer Bazaar’s “First Now Next” questionnaire

Gemma Chan first gained our attention in Crazy Rich Asians in 2018, stealing the screen as the glamorous, composed socialite Astrid. Chan imbued this character with a compelling emotional depth beneath the stoic exterior, a skill she honed during years of stage performance and her starring role in the British sci-fi show Humans.

Now Chan wants to produce films, to spend more time in the creative process leading up to filming. Her first film, Josephine, which she also stars in, is out on November 20. Directed by Beth de Araújo, the movie orbits around the titular Josephine, an eight-year-old girl played by the scene-stealing poppet Mason Reeves, and her parents, Claire (Chan) and Damien (Channing Tatum). One morning, when Josephine is running through the woods with her father, she witnesses a rape in the park before her father manages to intervene and save the woman. Josephine grapples with emotions and trauma she doesn’t understand, as her parents desperately try to provide care in opposing ways. Chan delivers a deeply intimate performance, drawing inspiration from the inner strength of the women in her family.

This summer, Chan also starred in Peacock’s show of the summer, Five Star Weekend, based on the novel by Elin Hilderbrand. She joined an all-star cast including Chloë Sevigny, Regina Hall, Jennifer Garner, and D’Arcy Carden. It tells the story of Hollis Shaw (Garner), a recent widow who decides to invite four friends from different stages of her life to her home in Nantucket for a luxurious, healing weekend.

Harper’s Bazaar’s questionnaire series “First, Now, Next” dives into the past, present, and future of some of our favorite creatives, spotlighting the moments and people that have influenced them. We touched base with Chan ahead of Jospehine’s release to talk about working with Channing Tatum, her time in Los Angeles unsuccessfully auditioning for pilots, and her dreams of running a cat sanctuary.

I would say the first film that I watched multiple times was The Sound of Music. We first watched it as a family when I was four years old. I would be imagining myself as one of the Von Trapp children and thought Christopher Plummer was amazing. He’s this terrifying but sophisticated guy who’s standing up to fascism. What’s not to like about that? Later in life, I would say the film that really spoke to me and made me want to become a storyteller was Secrets and Lies by Mike Leigh. What he managed to do there, and the emotional truth that his performers gave, was just extraordinary.

I was really fortunate that one of my first jobs was Secret Diary of a Call Girl. I met some people who are still my very good friends—Billie Piper and Lily James, as well as the director, Wayne Yip, British-Chinese like myself. The four of us became really fast friends, and we’re close to this day. It’s been a solace to have really solid friends from such an early stage in the industry.

A key one was in the early 2010s, when I first started going to Los Angeles for pilot season and auditioning for a whole bunch of American network shows. I got pretty close on a few, and I tested for some, but I never landed one. The whole trajectory of my life would have been different if I booked one of those jobs. I went back to the U.K., and I did more theater and more British TV. I ended up doing a show called Humans, which I absolutely loved, and I would say that was a pretty key point.

Later in my career, it’s been a little harder to find roles since turning 40. It’s part of the reason why I have loved starting to produce and being part of the creative process much earlier and finding the stories and getting to work on something like Josephine, which I also produced.

Some of them are really, really fun. They ran the whole gamut, from outright multi-cam sitcom to procedural drama. It was crazy, doing six auditions a day, driving all over town, having to memorize 10 pages for each. It was a real task. You get to the room and look around, and you’re one of however many actors sitting there, and everyone’s eyeing each other up. There was one audition that I went to and was waiting. I’d been up all night learning these lines, and I could literally hear the casting director offering the job on the phone to another actor who had been there earlier that day. I thought, “Oh my God, what am I doing?”

When I was 16, success meant losing my virginity, finding a way to stay out all night without my parents knowing, and getting a bit of distance from them. Nowadays, success looks like managing to have a good night’s sleep—getting eight hours—staying hydrated, and finding a way to spend as much time as possible with my parents. I want to spend every moment when I’m back at home in London.

There are a couple of things. They would constantly be on me about the importance of education and, eye roll, “I get it.” I am now the person who does that to my nephews, my little cousins: “Stay in school, kids!” Then, my dad had this particular way of loading the dishwasher. I’d say, “What are you talking about? There is no correct way!” I’ve discovered there is a correct way.

I was first sent the script back in 2019, and from the moment that I read it, I couldn’t get it out of my head. The truth sang from every page. I had a meeting with Beth, and she articulated her vision and what she wanted to do. I thought it was so unusual and daring and audacious. I believed in her vision. Then the pandemic happened. We were going to shoot it, and then the strikes happened. It’s been a really winding road, and it feels like a small miracle that we finally managed to get it made.

For me, the character of Claire really resonated. I’ve been fortunate enough to do so many different things in my career, but I’ve never gotten to play a role like hers. I saw a lot of myself in her. I see a lot of my own mother in her; my sister too. She has this real emotional intuition, and she’s not the loudest voice in the room, but she has the inner core of strength. She’s a survivor. Getting to play her was an amazing challenge of stripping everything back and getting to the truth of the story and the script.

It’s interesting because Claire and Damien have been raised in different ways, and they’re carrying their pasts with them. It changes how they approach parenting their daughter. I would say at the beginning of the film, they’re a unified front and haven’t really been tested. Then this thing happens where they both deeply love their daughter and want the best for her, but they don’t agree on how to do that. The first cracks start to appear. With Claire, it was about going into her background. She speaks a bit about her own relationship with her mother, about how they never spoke about difficult things and she felt a real distance from her. I’d say Claire is someone whose instincts are right for the most part, but she doesn’t always follow through on them. I had points where I wanted to shake both parents and go, “What are you doing?!” But then, I’m empathizing with them because they’re just two flawed human beings trying to do their best for their child. There is no handbook for it.

I’ve grown up and been a fan of Channing’s for a long time. If you think you know him from movies like Magic Mike and 21 Jump Street, you really don’t. What he brings to the role—the humor and the complete naturalness and spontaneity. He also brings such a level of vulnerability, and people will be really surprised by it. Before we started filming, Beth, he, and I spoke a lot about our own upbringings and our parents. He has a daughter, and that’s part of the reason why he was drawn to the script. We talked a lot about that and spent some time with Mason, who plays Josephine. We didn’t have to try; we could ease with each other. We hung out in the park. Channing and Mason got their nails done at the salon together. We went for dim sum. We did regular things, so she was comfortable. Hopefully, you believe in us as a family unit.

It’s not in my closet, sadly, but if I had to wear one outfit, it would be the pink Valentino dress I wore to the Oscars in 2019. It had pockets. It had volume. It was comfortable. I kept snacks in the pockets for the ceremony. Bury me in that dress. It’s dreamy, and I don’t know where it is.

The last thing I splurged on was a Steinway piano for myself. That’s amazing. Best decision I’ve ever made. Why didn’t I buy myself a good piano sooner? Something I will never spend money on is expensive mascara because you go through it at such a rate. You might as well just get a good drugstore mascara.

That was a dream getting to work with those women. None of us had met each other before; we had a blast between every take. We’d be in our little huddle of chairs, sharing the hot goss, going right in. It felt really, really special. They’re a brilliant bunch of women. Regina Hall made me laugh so much that I peed myself a little bit on set.

People really responded to this show about women and female friendship. There’s no murder. There’s nothing to solve in that sense, but it’s just about their lives and the day-to-day. People really relate to that, and obviously you have the aspirational element of this amazing house in Nantucket and the food porn.

Gigi is diabolical. Let’s be honest about that. Obviously, you’re not meant to judge your character, and when I read the scripts and approached it, obviously I wanted to empathize with her. Hopefully, you understand a bit of where she’s coming from. Then I watched a little bit of the show and was like, “Oh my God, she is insane for going on this weekend!” Absolutely insane.

She felt on the defensive. She did fall in love. She never wanted to. She tried to break it off. She was pursued. It takes two to tango and, sadly, the other party is deceased. She has to take the entire blame for it. I hope that she’s not villainized completely. I feel that Becca, the writer, and Erin, the author, both wanted her to be a fully dimensional human being. Although there is that secret, she has a genuine connection with these women. She genuinely grows to care about all of them, including Hollis. That is not an act. She is a complicated woman.

Celine Song, Denis Villeneuve, Chloe Dumont. They’re all so talented. I’d absolutely die to work with any of them.

That’s an easy question! I’d open a cat sanctuary and rescue all the cats that I meet. I’m about to adopt another cat from Puglia. He’s five years old, and we’re actually rescuing another kitten that my sister found abandoned. She’s coming from France next month, and we’re going to introduce them, and hopefully they’ll get along.

#6833. Yes, Katie and Victor Were Flirting on ‘The Traitors: New Blood’—and They Dished All About Their First Date

Nothing says romance like trying not to get murdered on reality TV.

The Traitors may be a game of deceit, but Victor Vollbrechthausen and Katie Fites’ flirtation? That was apparently very real.

As the first civilian-only season of The Traitors: New Blood came to a close, host Alan Cumming asked Katie, “Is there a possible date on the cards for you and Victor?”

She seemed tainted by the fact that Victor had banished her, jokingly saying, “No date for you,” until he kept the banter going and claimed that he “earned it” after finding out she was a Traitor. Now, the star-crossed Faithful and Traitor romance has seemingly made its way out of Alan’s Scottish castle.

During a joint postseason interview with Entertainment Weekly, Victor and Katie revealed they followed through on their plans and actually went on a first date.

“We have gone on that date. After incessant begging, let the kids back home know that persistence works,” Katie joked, before revealing that it was “great.”

“It was fun to get to know each other outside of her trying to murder me, me trying not to get murdered. It was nice to take a moment just for us,” Victor, who played as a Faithful while Katie was one of the season’s Traitors, told the outlet.

As for what their first date entailed? Well, there was certainly no discussion about their time on the show, since Victor banned any mention of it. And, honestly, who can blame him? After all, he was onto Katie’s strategy to potentially target him for murder.

While her crush on Victor helped her game, Katie admitted that she genuinely enjoyed hanging out with him and even grew close to him throughout the season. She also revealed that she didn’t immediately kill Victor off because she wasn’t aware of his suspicions toward her—though he had been side-eyeing her earlier on than she realized.

“I think there could be,” Katie added. “But we’ll see. Gonna have to keep persisting.”

#6832. How to Play GTA VI All Weekend Without Destroying Your Body

The day has finally arrived. Grand Theft Auto VI is here, and you’ve taken that “sick day” to cause chaos on the streets of Vice City. After more than 10 years of waiting, you’re finally back in one of the franchise’s most classic locations, this time with an open world to explore. It’s not hyperbole to call this the most highly anticipated game in history. With more than 17 million preorders, that’s a lot of people who will be running from the police while trying to avoid (or rack up) six stars.

If you’re one of the many fans who’ve cleared out their schedule to binge the game this weekend, you’ll want to make sure you emerge from your haze with extremities that still function.

“The metaphor we like to share with pro players or casual gamers is the concept of the health bar,” says Dr. Matthew Hwu, owner of physical therapy clinic 1 Health Point in Monrovia, California. “The muscles and tendons of your wrist, hand, thumb, as you move the analog stick, only have so much capacity or ‘max HP’ that they can handle throughout the day.”

Below, we spoke with Dr. Hwu and other veteran gamers about how to keep your “HP” bar full for your whole weekend without developing carpal tunnel.

Playing a game for long hours puts the soft tissues in your hands and arms into overdrive. When you ramp things up, you’ll feel sore and fatigued. The most common version of this is “tendon-based problems of the wrist and hand,” says Dr. Hwu, who has spent more than 11 years working with e-sports players to help them recover from injuries. As GTA VI will be available on PlayStation 5 and Xbox Series S and X, most people will be using a controller, which puts the thumbs under a lot of stress as people “control the analog stick to flip left, up, right, down; press A, B, X, Y.” The most common gaming injuries are gamer’s thumb and tennis elbow, according to the Cleveland Clinic.

So, don’t forget to take breaks. Dr. Hwu recommends at least a five-minute break for every 90 minutes played, though younger players are more resilient. If you want to push it, the key is taking longer breaks afterwards. “Two hours, 10-minute break, three hours, 15-minute break,” Dr. Hwu says. “If you want to be degenerate, you can be a smart degen.” You can also be strategic about scheduling more downtime after sequences with higher APM (actions per minute), that are more intensive on hand muscles. In essence, go touch grass after a high-stakes bank robbery.

That said, after more than a decade of waiting for what our reviewer described as “the most technically and artistically impressive game world ever made,” your instinct may be to lock in. So it’s wise to plan for breaks ahead of time. Set alarms on your phone or watch. If one goes off in the middle of a mission, commit to taking a break after crashing that helicopter. Make sure you eat and stay hydrated, and move your body, whether that’s a neighborhood walk or just a short twirl around your apartment before your next mission.

“It’s basically just your muscle and tendon tissue recovery, it’ll restore a little bit of HP over time,” Dr. Hwu says. “When you go out and walk outdoors, that can help with your overall circulation, nervous system as well. So you don’t feel like that meme where you come out of the cave and you’re like a ghost.”

Just like in other forms of training, stretching is a key part of your self-care arsenal for long hours of gaming. To loosen up for a long stretch of car-hijacking, do a few stretches before turning on your console, and during cut scenes. Your body will tell you what areas feel tight and could use some attention. But don’t forget those thumb muscles. “Bring your thumb away from the next finger, stretching it down,” Dr. Hwu says. “Every time you’re flicking back, left, or right, you might be using these muscles.”

You’ll also want to stretch your index fingers after long hours of beating ass with a crowbar while riding your motorcycle. “Every time you press your trigger,” Dr. Hwu says, “you’re using the extensors, the top side of your fingers, but also the flexors to push down.” You can also work on relaxing your forearm muscles, by extending your arm out and pushing your palm downward.

After 13 years, and a budget rumored to rival that of Dubai’s Burj Khalifa skyscraper, the new Grand Theft Auto is finally here. Our critic's verdict? It's a tour de force that reimagines what a video game can be.

Don’t forget your neck and upper back, which can feel stiff from holding onto stress after high speed chases and parachute combat. It’s so easy to load tension onto those upper traps as you shrimp further into your recliner while bird watching, or scoring a pair of black half-rim sunglasses and Eris sneakers (definitely not Nikes) for Jason (no judgment here).

“I will do small things: roll my neck from side to side, stretch my traps by sitting on one hand and pulling my head [in the] opposite direction,” Calliope Ryder, platform director at GYLD, previously a producer on Tales of the Shire, says. “[I] tense my calves in spurts to help with blood flow, or stand up and let my body fold at the hips while swaying side to side with my arms crossed. Again, alerts to remind me to do it are necessary or I'll go hours without noticing.”

Meanwhile, using a heating pad or an ice pack on a sore area (like your lower back) can help with any nagging dull pain you’re feeling. Heat loosens up stiff muscles, while ice can help dull the achy sensation. They won’t build your long-term strength, but they’ll make you feel better in the moment.

If you’re old enough for back pain, then you’re old enough to have to consider video game ergonomics. Wherever you’re gaming, Dr. Hwu’s recommendation is simple: Sit with your legs at a 90-degree angle, feet planted on the ground, back appropriately supported. Then put a pillow on your lap and rest your arms on it. This helps prevent forearm strain and hunching over. You can even haul your expensive Aeron from your office into the living room, rather than rotting on the sofa and ruining your back.

There are also a number of helpful gaming peripherals. Maddy Myers, editor in chief and co-founder of video game publication Mothership, uses a specific gaming pillow from a brand called Mechanism. “The pillow allows you to have the handheld device suspended in the air in a comfortable, adjustable spot so that you don’t tire out your arms from holding it up,” she says. “You can also put a controller on there.”

Using the device helps her get through play stretches of eight hours a day on the weekends, “while taking breaks to eat, do chores, stretch.” It’s been especially invaluable during high-intensity work periods. “I have to play GTA 6 for my job, so I am really going to be relying on these tools to keep my arms and body feeling as close to normal as possible,” she says.

Don’t let a terribly designed controller be the reason you develop carpal tunnel. Julia Lee, section lead for video game guides at Polygon, plays at least two hours of games a day, and will sometimes fit in 10-hour sessions. Her wrists started hurting and “those flat [Nintendo Switch] Joy-Cons were directly to blame,” she says. “I ended up getting a Nintendo Switch Pro Controller (which has a much more ergonomic shape and grip), and that helped alleviate pain a lot.” She also reminds herself not to hold the controller so tight, as it causes pain.

As Dr. Hwu explains, some controllers have staggered sticks, while others have an inline design. It all comes down to individual comfort and muscular endurance. He’s helped pro Call of Duty players modify their analog sticks, and has coached people to avoid death-gripping the controller. “If you use a claw grip position, an awkward grip, or a modded controller, that might lead to more stress for certain muscles in your wrist and hand,” he says.

If your hands are really killing you, there’s also the option of switching to another controller style altogether. “Moving two to four of my most awkward hand-based actions from my hands to my feet allows me to play longer,” John Warren, chief marketing officer and cofounder of Glydr, a foot-based video game controller, says. Warren has minicore disease, which causes muscle weakness. “Anytime you have an opportunity to stop overloading your hands while you play, you give your hands a little reprieve,” he says, “it makes your high-precision hand actions more accurate.”

Lastly, if you have existing injuries, and your medical provider has prescribed a brace, actually follow their guidance for how and when to use it. For my part, I use thumb braces called the Push Metagrips, to help support my floppy thumb joints anytime I plan on playing video games for long stretches.

So you’ve done all this work to binge GTA 6, and you’re feeling solid but a little sore. But you want to play for longer periods, snipe with greater accuracy, and fuck around with less fatigue. Consider going to the gym.

Doing exercises for those small muscles and tendons can help you build up that tolerance, like any other form of weight and endurance training. “When you rest, ice, massage, kinesio tape, you know, passive interventions, bracing, all of these things can restore your HP,” Dr. Hwu says. “It does nothing to change the size of your health bar, which represents the tissue’s capacity or your endurance. The way we do that is you can do specific exercises to build up or create a buffer for how much you’re going to handle.”

For any chronic pain, injuries, or conditions, it’s important to work with a physical therapist. I’m currently working with one for repetitive stress injury recovery, and doing exercises specific to playing video games. I do slow wrist curls with a two-pound weight, rotator cuff exercises with a resistance band, and squeeze an egg-shaped stress ball for thumb and forearm endurance. I also train my shoulders and core.

For those who aren’t managing any injuries, training those finger and arm muscles make it easier to game for longer periods. Consistency is key, and so is having reasonable expectations. Dr. Hwu recommends starting once a day, three to four times a week, “on a day where you’re not doing a lot, and then you’ll learn how your body responds to doing exercises within the context of your specific life, and you can make modifications to scale up the aggressiveness or scale down,” he says. As for seeing progress, “it takes four to six weeks for muscles, eight weeks for tendons.”

And don’t forget the stabilizer muscles in your shoulders, which hold up your upper body and support your arms. “I focus on ‘chest opening’ exercises that strengthen your back,” Ryder says. “I tend towards curving forward so I consciously strengthen the 'opposite' muscles so they pull my shoulders back a bit.” She also does chin tucks, to strengthen her deep neck flexors.

Strength training is also a great way to tackle back pain, another common complaint after gaming for hours. “A few years ago I started having extreme back pain when I sat in my office chair,” Gita Jackson, cofounder of video game publication Aftermath, says. They play one to two hours of games a day, but often play marathon sessions when new games come out. “The pain was excruciating, and sometimes my legs would get so stiff I couldn’t walk. Recovery started with physical therapy, and then a new ergonomic chair,” and now they regularly attend pilates.

“I really had no idea that if I wanted to continue playing noodley strategy games without pain that I would have to be a person who works out multiple times a week,” they say.

Maybe you’ve done a little too much, and your arms start to feel heavy as bricks, with thumbs that ache for hours. Even with the best precautions in place, it can happen to any of us.

“If it gets to a point where cumulatively, over days, symptoms are getting worse, maybe reflect and identify what you could change,” Dr. Hwu says. “If things are consistently sharp, or you start to feel some weakness, where when you go and try to pick up something, you’ll want to drop it. That’s a sign that it may be time to make a change in some of the variables that you can control.”

In 2002, weeks before the release of Grand Theft Auto: Vice City, the journalist David Kushner met Sam and Dan Houser, plus Rockstar co-founder Terry Donovan, at their SoHo loft. It would be their last and only in-depth sit-down before they decided that the world’s most successful video game required little explanation.

This is when paying attention to your daily habits is key. Instead of freaking out over hand pain, and deciding you need to quit playing cold turkey, you can instead think about what you did during the day that might have contributed to your discomfort. “How much you’re playing, what intensity you’re playing, are you taking breaks, are you exercising, how are you responding to the pain?” Dr. Hwu says. These are all factors in why you might be having pain, and all things to consider modifying. If it truly is tendon fatigue, then the key is finding the right volume of use, rather than quitting altogether.

There are also some signs to look out for that may require medical intervention. Dr. Hwu pointed me to 1 Health Point’s website, which suggests seeking a doctor’s opinion if your wrist looks swollen or bruised, if you can’t move or bear any weight, if your symptoms worsen or spread with rest, or if you experience constant hand numbness, especially if it comes with weakness.

Gaming for long hours ultimately comes down to using your common sense alongside your precious free will. Pay attention to your habits. Commit to taking breaks and stretching between bank robberies and doomscrolling through Snapmatic (in-game social media). Do some preventative exercises while planning out your next mission—with a soundtrack that includes Bad Bunny and Dua Lipa, as well as some ’80s bangers, you’ve already got the music for it. Your hands and arms will thank you, even if the regular people of Leonida probably won’t.

#6831. Plestia Alaqad Sees the World

The photojournalist talks about the making of her latest book, The Eyes of Gaza

“The hardest part was having something as intimate and personal as a diary become public,” says Plestia Alaqad of her decision to publish her diary-memoir, The Eyes of Gaza. The book details the harrowing first weeks after October 7 in Gaza as a young Palestinian journalist. Alaqad studied new media and journalism at Eastern Mediterranean University in Cyprus before returning to her home in 2022. Intending to educate the world about her city, she did not know she would end up reporting the first stages of the genocide.

Alaqad’s reporting situated herself as a voice for the ordinary. In the first few days, she spoke to her neighbors and local children. Her reporting appeared mostly on her Instagram account, which grew to a little over 2 million followers, with many of her images republished by the BBC, The New York Times, PBS NewsHour, and The Washington Post. Even through the chaos and the expulsion of over 100,000 Gazans in the first few days, according to UNHCR, which Alaqad also recounts in her memoir, there are moments of tenderness caught by her and everyday Gazans she would meet and talk to. Her style of reporting is grounded in a sense of familiarity, narrating the everyday in extraordinary circumstances. She made connections with people in a way that makes you feel like you were alongside her, drinking tea with the local tetas and jiddos, watching the violence and the world collapse around you.

Alaqad left Gaza for Lebanon, where she is completing her master’s degree. During the course of our interview, she spoke openly with me about the loneliness of the isolation of being a witness, forever changed by the devastation.

One of my favorite things about Gaza is the people and the sense of community. For example, the relationship between my neighbors and I. I’ve been living in the Al -Waleed Building since I was almost four years old, and I know all my neighbors. Like Dana, who lived next door next to me—it was like having a sister and best friend nearby at all times. Whenever I needed anything, I just knocked at Dana’s door. If I needed help picking out an outfit, if I needed help replying to messages, even if I was just bored and I wanted someone to hang out with, it was always Dana who I turned to. There’s something about Gaza that just is home to me. There is simplicity and a love for life in Gaza that I haven’t seen elsewhere in the world. So being born and raised there always meant having loving and supportive people around me.

There’s more to us than being occupied. We’re full of talent and art we never got the time to make, because we’re too busy worrying if the people we love will still be alive tomorrow. That’s what pushes me to keep writing.

I’m interested in writing and telling stories of people. I recently interviewed Renad Atallah and wrote a piece about her for The New Arab. Also, one of my favorite pieces I wrote was an interview with Alaa Abu Diab, a one-of-a-kind comedian, for The National.

As of recently, I started my newsletter on Substack, where I share a personal essay every week on Gaza, girlhood, friendship, and everything in between. My ultimate hope would be to tell stories about a free Palestine one day. And as a journalist, I’ll always see my work as incomplete without going back to Gaza and continuing to tell Palestinian stories from the ground. And hopefully work on an international level and tell stories of people who suffered because of war and conflicts in other countries.

I enjoy reading nonfiction books and memoirs because I love learning about people and their experiences. I remember reading Anne Frank’s diary in middle school.… I remember how it made me feel and the impact it had on me.

I have also always been drawn to personal writings, like Franz Kafka’s letters and the letters between Ghassan Kanafani and Ghada al-Samman. I think I’m a very curious person by nature, and I’m interested in understanding what is happening in people’s minds, and if I get the chance to read strangers’ diaries, I wouldn't say no. That curiosity is something that inspired me.

Growing up, I always had a diary. It was me and my purple diary against the world.… For me, journaling has always been my safe space in the world. It’s the one place where I can write whatever I want without feeling judged. That’s honestly why I wanted to create Neya [Alaqad’s creative brand], which is an Arabic word that means intention. The first product will be a journal, of course, and it will come in four different colors. Each journal has a small, kind reminder on the hardcover and little sweet quotes throughout the pages because I wanted to create something that is personal and safe, something you would want to come back to and share your thoughts in. For me, journaling has always been where I can reconnect with myself. I hope Neya can become that for other people too: a small space where they can pause, reflect, and reconnect with themselves.

The hardest part was having something as intimate and personal as a diary become public. Something that was once only mine suddenly was in the hands of people from all around the world. I always try to think about the impact it could have on others, and I’m grateful that this time we get to tell our own stories rather than having our stories told on our behalf. I’m also grateful for the readers around the world who have read The Eyes of Gaza and are making the effort to educate themselves about Palestine.

I often feel like I am living in two worlds. On one hand, I have the opportunity to do things that many people back home cannot access freely, such as pursuing my education and continuing my master’s degree. On the other hand, I carry the weight of knowing what is happening to my people and my land, without having any superpower to stop it.

I always try to stay honest and true to myself, and I try to take people through this journey with me online. I share the contradictions, the pain, and the moments of hope. What it’s like trying to process the experience of having your homeland bombed, your city erased, and your people murdered, while also surviving and trying to make sense of the world outside. It is something that is difficult to put into words.

It feels like time has frozen in Gaza, while the rest of the world continues moving forward. And for those of us away from home, we are constantly trying to live between these two realities.

Gaza is a place where contradictions exist side by side. You see unimaginable grief, but you also see people trying to find meaning and strength in the middle of that suffering.

The Eyes of Gaza captures these contradictions. On one page, sadness may take over, while on another, hope emerges. Ultimately, I believe that when people experience powerful emotions, they are often moved to take action.

#6830. The Watches of Grand Theft Auto 6 (and Their Real-Life Inspiration)

From Rolex Day-Dates to Casio digitals, a guide to the timepieces of Vice City.

For one glorious moment, the world appears to be united. Grand Theft Auto VI has finally arrived, to near-universal acclaim, with millions of people abandoning work, school, and presumably all their other responsibilities to explore the streets of Vice City. In these divided times, it's enough to warm the cockles of your heart.

And amid all the fast cars, guns, nightclubs, and criminal capers, there's something else we pay attention to here at Esquire HQ: the watches!

Rockstar Games' latest blockbuster reportedly features 34 "collectible" luxury watches scattered throughout its fictional version of Florida. But that's only part of the story. GTA VI also has its own watch brands, although some of the designs look ... suspiciously familiar.

There's Crowex (a dead ringer for Rolex), Takio (distinctly Casio-adjacent), and Covgari, whose watches owe more to Audemars Piguet than the Italian-o jeweller its name suggests.

From gold Day-Dates to pocket-money Casio digitals, here are some of our favorite real-world watches that appear to have inspired the game's timepieces.

The gold Crowex is a near dead ringer for the Rolex Day-Date 40 (ref. 228238). The fluted bezel, black dial, and day window at 12 o'clock are all present and correct, although the bracelet differs somewhat from Rolex's iconic President. The Day-Date celebrates its 70th anniversary this year. Evidently, it remains the universal symbol of having made it, even in the virtual world.

The silver chronograph worn by a character overlooking Vice City has more than a passing resemblance to the mighty Daytona. The round case, contrasting sub-dials, and metal bracelet suggest that famous racing chronograph. As we know, the Daytona is one of the world's most sought-after sports watches, making it a natural fit for GTA's world of expensive cars, conspicuous consumption, and bling.

Brian Heder, a veteran drug smuggler, wears a metal sports watch that certainly seems to recall Rolex's Sub. There's the substantial case, dark dial, and the bracelet. The Submariner has been a fixture of luxury watchmaking since 1953, and its more ostentatious steel-and-gold versions would be right at home on the streets of Vice City.

Jason Duval, one of GTA VI's two protagonists, wears a chunky digital watch resembling something from Casio's Pro Trek range. The large circular display, prominent buttons and rugged case certainly suggest its something from the range of the Japanese juggernaut. A more practical choice than the gold watches favored by some of his associates.

Not everyone in Vice City is wearing a gold Rollie lookalike. The Takio F-BLAST is an unmistakable nod to Casio's inexpensive digital classics, particularly the bestselling F-91W. It has the familiar rectangular display, black dial surround, and simple, functional design. There's even a more colorful version, with a red case and two-tone metal bracelet, spotted in the game's Hamlet Thrift store.

As spotted by watch Instagram account @geesgshock.
